Conceptual Modeling 
Topic 1 
Data vs. Information 
Conceptual Modeling The Framework of Data Abstraction 
■ Data: Raw, unorganized facts that need to be processed (e.g., a list of numbers or dates). It represents the "Extensional" aspect of a database. 
■ Information: Data that has been processed, interpreted, and organized to provide meaning (e.g., an average grade or a financial report). 
■The Goal of Modeling: To create a structure that allows raw data to be transformed into reliable information for decision-making. 
■Metadata: Data about data; the descriptions of the data structures and constraints that give data its context.

3/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Framework of Data Abstraction 
The ANSI-SPARC Architecture 
■ Proposed in 1975 to standardize database management systems (DBMS). 
■ Defines a Three-Level Architecture to separate user applications from the physical database. 
■ Objectives: 
■Support multiple individual user views. 
■ Hide physical storage details from users. 
■ Allow the DBA to change storage structures without affecting users. 
■ Ensure the "Internal" state is independent of the "External" perception. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
4/1260 
Conceptual Modeling 
The Framework of Data Abstraction 
The Three Levels of Abstraction 
1. External Level (User Views): The level closest to the users. Describes how different 
groups of users perceive the data. 
2. Conceptual Level (Community View): The logical structure of the entire database. It 
describes what data is stored and the relationships among the data. 
3. Internal Level (Physical View): The level closest to physical storage. Describes how 
data is actually stored on the storage medium.

6/1260 
Conceptual Modeling 
The Framework of Data Abstraction 
Internal Level: The Physical View 
■ Focuses on efficiency and storage. 
■ Involves low-level data structures: 
■ Memory allocation and block sizes. 
■Record sequencing and ordering. 
■ Path specifications and indexing (e.g., B-trees, Hashing). 
■ Data compression and encryption techniques. 
The goal is to optimize hardware utilization and response time.

2025/26/2 
7/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Framework of Data Abstraction 
Conceptual Level: The Community View 
■The Logical Middle Ground. 
■ Describes the entire information model of the organization. 
■ Key elements: 
■ All entities, their attributes, and their relationships. 
■ Semantic constraints (rules the data must follow). 
■Security and integrity information. 
■ Hides physical complexity while providing a unified view of all data. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
8/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Framework of Data Abstraction 
External Level: User Views 
■ Tailored to specific user needs (e.g., HR view, Accounting view). 
■ A single database has many external schemas. 
■ Benefits: 
■ Security: Users only see what they are authorized to see. 
■ Simplicity: Users deal with a simplified subset of the data. 
■ Customization: Different users can have different names for the same data. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
9/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Framework of Data Abstraction 
The Mechanism of Mappings 
■Mappings are the "translation layers" that allow the levels to communicate. 
■ External/Conceptual Mapping: Defines the correspondence between a specific user view and the global logical schema. 
■ Conceptual/Internal Mapping: Defines how the logical entities and relationships are represented in physical storage. 
■If a schema at one level changes, the mapping must be updated to keep the levels above it consistent.

2025/26/2 
10/1260 
Data Independence 
Conceptual Modeling The Framework of Data Abstraction 
■ One of the primary advantages of the Three-Level Architecture. 
■ Definition: The ability to change the schema at one level of a database system without having to change the schema at the next higher level. 
■Two distinct types: 
1. Logical Data Independence. 2. Physical Data Independence. 
User Applications 
Logical Independence 
Conceptual Logic 
Change schema without rewriting apps. 
Physical Independence 
Change representation without changing logic. 
Internal Storage 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
11/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Framework of Data Abstraction 
Logical Data Independence 
■The ability to change the Conceptual Schema without affecting the External Schemas or application programs. 
■ Scenario: Adding a new entity (e.g., "Social Media Handles") to the database should not require re-writing existing reports that only look at "Names" and "Addresses." 
■Changes to the community view are absorbed by the mapping between the External and Conceptual levels. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
12/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Framework of Data Abstraction 
Physical Data Independence 
■The ability to change the Internal Schema without affecting the Conceptual Schema. 
■ Scenario: Switching from an older hard drive to a high-speed SSD or changing an indexing method to speed up searches. 
■The logical definition of the data (what an "Employee" is) remains identical, even if the storage method changes. 
■ Changes are absorbed by the mapping between the Conceptual and Internal levels.

14/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model 
Introduction to ER Modeling 
■ Origin: Proposed by Peter Chen in 1976 as a way to unify network and relational database views. 
■ Purpose: A high-level conceptual data model used to describe the "real world" as a collection of basic objects and their associations. 
■ Key Components: 
■ Entities: The "Nouns" (Objects). 
■ Attributes: The "Adjectives" (Properties). 
■ Relationships: The "Verbs" (Associations). 
■ Semantic Power: Captures business rules and data requirements in a format understandable by both technical and non-technical stakeholders.

17/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model 
Entities: Type, Instance, and Set 
■ Entity Type (Intension): The template or blueprint. It defines the schema, naming the attributes that all entities of this category share. 
■ Example: The concept of a STUDENT (possessing an ID, Name, and Major). 
■ Entity Instance (Entity): A specific, unique object in the real world that conforms to an Entity Type. 
Example: A person named 'Alice' with ID '101'. 
■ Entity Set (Extension): The collection of all entity instances of a particular type currently stored in the database. 
■ Example: The group of 5,000 students currently enrolled in the university. 
Theoretical Note 
In ER diagrams, a rectangle labeled STUDENT technically represents the Entity Type, but we often refer to it as the Entity Set because it logically contains the instances. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
18/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model 
Attribute Taxonomy: Simple vs. Composite 
■ Simple (Atomic) Attributes: Cannot be divided into smaller components (e.g., Gender, Salary). 
■Composite Attributes: Can be divided into sub-parts with independent meanings. 
■ Example: Address can be divided into Street, City, Zip. 
■ Benefit: Users can query the whole address or just the City. 
■ Modeling Choice: We model as composite when the hierarchy of data needs to be preserved for clarity. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
19/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model 
Attribute Taxonomy: Single-valued vs. Multi-valued 
■Single-valued: Holds one value for a single entity (e.g., SSN, Date_of_Birth). 
■Multi-valued: Can hold a set of values for a single entity. 
■ Example: Phone_Numbers (a person may have home, cell, and work numbers). 
■Notation: Double-line oval. 
■Relational Constraint: Relational databases (1NF) do not support multi-valued attributes directly; they must be mapped to separate tables (Topic 5). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
20/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model 
Attribute Taxonomy: Derived Attributes 
■ Definition: An attribute whose value is calculated from other attributes or related 
entities. 
■ Example: Age derived from Current_Date - Date_of_Birth; Total_Price derived from Quantity * Unit_Price. 
■Notation: Dashed-line oval. 
■Trade-off: 
■Storage: Saves space if not stored (calculated on-the-fly). 
■ Performance: Saves CPU cycles if pre-calculated and stored (materialization). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
21/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model 
Attribute Roles: Identifying vs. Non-identifying 
■ Identifying Attributes (Keys): 
■ Values that uniquely distinguish one entity instance from all others in the set. 
■Rule: No two instances can share the same value for an identifying attribute. 
■Notation: Underlined text in ER diagrams. 
■ Example: Social_Security_Number in a PERSON entity. 
■ Non-identifying Attributes (Descriptors): 
■ Attributes that describe properties or characteristics of an entity but do not guarantee uniqueness. 
■ Example: Date_of_Birth, Hair_Color, or Salary. 
Composite Identifiers 
Sometimes a single attribute is insufficient. A Composite Key consists of multiple attributes that together ensure uniqueness (e.g., FirstName + LastName + Birth Date). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
22/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model 
Identity Sources: Natural vs. Artificial Keys 
■ Natural Keys: 
■ Attributes that exist in the real world and have business meaning. 
■ Examples: ISBN (Books), VIN (Vehicles), EmailAddress (Users). 
■Risk: Real-world data can change or contain duplicates (e.g., people change. emails). 
■ Artificial (Surrogate) Keys: 
■ System-generated identifiers created solely for the database. 
■ Examples: Auto-increment ID, UUID (Universally Unique ID). 
■ Benefit: They are Immutable (never change) and Meaningless (unaffected by business rule changes). 
Feature

23/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model 
Strong Entities: Identity Independence 
■ Definition: An entity that stands alone. It possesses a Primary Key (PK) derived solely from its own attributes. 
■ Characteristic: It does not require the existence of any other specific entity instance to be uniquely identified. 
■ Visual: Single-lined Rectangle. 
Example (Example: A University Building) 
■ Entity: BUILDING. 
■ Attributes: Building_Name, Address, Year Built. 
■Logic: "Science Hall" is a unique identifier. Even if the building is empty (no rooms, no people), it exists as a distinct, identifiable record in the database. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
24/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model 
Weak Entities: Identity Dependency 
■ Definition: An entity that cannot be identified by its own attributes alone. It relies on an Owner Entity. 
■The Identity Recipe: 
PK_Weak = PK_Owner + Partial Key (Discriminator) 
■ Visual: Double-lined Rectangle. Connected via a Double-lined Diamond (Identifying Relationship). 
Example (Example: A Room inside a Building) 
■ Entity: ROOM. 
■ Attributes: Room_Number, Capacity. 
■The Problem: There are fifty "Room 101"s on campus. 
■The Logic: To identify a room, we must know the Building. 
■ Identity: (Building_Name, Room_Number). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
25/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model 
Refactoring: Promoting Attributes 
■ Smell: An entity Project has a multi-valued attribute Locations. 
■ Analysis: Does a Location have its own properties (e.g., Address, Tax Rate)? 
■ Refactoring: 
■ Before: Project → {Location Names}. 
■ After: Project (M) → (N) Location (Entity). 
■Benefit: We can now track distinct information about the location itself, rather than just a string literal. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
26/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model 
The Mystery of Missing Values 
■The NULL state: Represents the absence of a value. 
■Meanings of NULL: 
1. Value Unknown: The value exists (e.g., Home_Phone), but we don't have it in the 
system. 
2. Value Missing: We don't know if a value exists or not. 
3. Not Applicable: The attribute is irrelevant for this specific entity (e.g., 
Apartment Number for a standalone house). 
■Computational Impact: Leads to Three-Valued Logic (3VL) in SQL. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
27/1260 
Relationship Theory 
Conceptual Modeling The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model 
■Relationship: An association among several entities (e.g., Student Alice enrolled in Course DB101). 
■Relationship Set: A mathematical relation on n > 2 entity sets. 
■ Attributes on Relationships: Sometimes data belongs to the association, not the entities. 
■ Example: Hours_Worked on the Works On relationship between Employee and Project. 
■ An employee has many hours, a project has many hours; the specific "8 hours" only exists where they meet. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
28/1260 
Degree of Relationship 
Conceptual Modeling The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model 
■ Unary (Recursive): Degree 1. An entity relates to itself. 
■ Binary: Degree 2. Most common. (e.g., Department has Employees). 
■Ternary: Degree 3. Three entities involved simultaneously. 
■ Example: A Physician prescribes a Drug to a Patient. 
■Note: This is not the same as three binary relationships (Physician-Drug, Drug-Patient, Physician-Patient). 
■N-ary: Generalization to any number of entities. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
29/1260 
Recursive Relationships 
Conceptual Modeling The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model 
■Concept: The same entity set participates in a relationship in different roles. 
■Role Names: Essential for distinguishing the participation. 
■ Examples: 
■ EMPLOYEE (Role: Manager) manages EMPLOYEE (Role: Subordinate). 
■ COURSE (Role: Prerequisite) is required for COURSE (Role: Advanced). 
■Traversal: Requires recursive queries (CTEs) in SQL. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
30/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model 
Associative Entities (Reification) 
■The "Gerund" Concept: Turning a relationship into an entity. 
■ Why Reify? 
When a relationship must participate in another relationship. 
■ When a relationship has its own life-cycle (independent of the participants). 
■ Example: 
■ Relationship: STUDENT enrolls in COURSE. 
■Reified: ENROLLMENT becomes an entity. Now, a FACULTY member can grade an ENROLLMENT. 
■ Notation: A rectangle enclosing a diamond. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
31/1260 
The Reification Process 
Conceptual Modeling The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model 
Initial State (Verb) 
■ Patient visits Doctor. 
■ Association has a date. 
Reified State (Noun) 
■ Patient → Visit ← Doctor. 
■ Visit is an entity. 
■ We can now add Diagnosis and Billing to the Visit. 
Theoretical Insight 
Reification transforms a predicate (logic) into a constant (object), allowing higher-order associations to be modeled in first-order structures. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
32/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model 
Refactoring: Resolving M:N Attributes 
■Smell: Relationship Student enrolled Course has an attribute Grade. 
■ Trigger: We need to track Grade Appeals and Audit History for that grade. 
■Refactoring: Reification. 
■Turn enrolled into Strong Entity Enrollment. 
■ Enrollment now has relationship Has_Appeal. 
■Rule: When a "link" needs its own "links," it must become a "node." 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
33/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model 
Case 1: Ternary Relationship vs. Three Binaries 
■ Scenario: A Physician prescribes a Drug to a Patient. 
■Why it's Ternary: The prescription represents a unique meeting of all three. 
■The Failure of Binary Decomposition: 
■Binary 1: Physician treats Patient. 
■Binary 2: Physician prescribes Drug. 
■Binary 3: Patient takes Drug. 
■The Gap: If Dr. Smith treats Alice, and Dr. Smith prescribes Penicillin, and Alice takes Penicillin, we still don't know if Dr. Smith was the one who prescribed it to her. 
■Lesson: Ternary relationships are irreducible when the association is an atomic event. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
34/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model 
Case 2: Multiple Relationships Between Entities 
■ Entities can be linked by more than one relationship type simultaneously. 
■ Example: AIRLINE and FLIGHT. 
1. Relationship A: Operates (An airline operates many flights). 
2. Relationship B: Owns (An airline owns the aircraft for the flight). 
3. Relationship C: Codeshares (An airline sells tickets for another's flight). 
■ Modeling Rule: Each relationship must be named uniquely and have its own cardinality/participation constraints. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
35/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model 
Case 3: Non-Transferable Relationships 
■In some domains, once an association is formed, it cannot change (Immutability). 
■ Example: BIRTH_RECORD and MOTHER. 
■ While a 1:N relationship, it is Non-transferable. 
■ER Challenge: Standard notation lacks an "immutability" flag. 
■ Solution: This is often handled at the Logical Level via ON UPDATE RESTRICT or triggers, but should be noted as a constraint in the conceptual diagram. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
36/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model 
Case 4: Overlapping Roles in Unary Relationships 
■Scenario: PART contains PART (Bill of Materials). 
■ A part can be both a "Parent" (assembly) and a "Child" (component). 
■Complexity: Can a part contain itself? 
■Logic: This requires a Cycle Constraint (A part cannot be a sub-component of itself, even indirectly). 
■This logic is a "Hidden Constraint" that standard ER diagrams cannot visualize without annotations. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
37/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model 
Case 5: Redundant vs. Essential Relationships 
■ Redundant: A path between A and C that can be fully derived from A-B and B-C. 
■ Essential: A path between A and C that carries unique semantic meaning. 
■ Example: 
■ Path 1: EMPLOYEE→ DEPARTMENT. 
■ Path 2: EMPLOYEE→ PROJECT→ DEPARTMENT. 
■If Path 1 represents "Permanent Org Unit" and Path 2 represents "Current Billable Location," both are essential. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
38/1260 
Conceptual Modeling The Entity-Relationship (ER) Model 
Refactoring: Demoting Relationships 
■ Smell: A 1:1 relationship where one side has no unique attributes. 
■ Scenario: Order (1, 1)→ (1, 1) Shipping Info. 
■ Analysis: If Shipping Info just contains (Address, Speed) and is deleted when Order is deleted... 
■Refactoring: Collapse Shipping Info into attributes of Order. 
■ Benefit: Reduced complexity; fewer joins; simpler diagram.

2025/26/2 
39/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Structural Constraints & Logic 
Introduction to Structural Constraints 
■ Purpose: To define the business rules that govern how entities are associated. 
■The Constraint Matrix: Every relationship is defined by two primary dimensions: 1. Cardinality Ratio (Max Cardinality): The maximum number of relationship 
instances an entity can participate in. 
2. Participation Constraint (Min Cardinality): Whether the existence of an entity 
depends on being related to another entity. 
■ Significance: These constraints dictate the schema implementation, specifically foreign key placement and NULL allowability. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
40/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Structural Constraints & Logic 
Cardinality Ratios (Max Cardinality) 
■ One-to-One (1:1): An entity in A is associated with at most one entity in B, and vice 
versa. 
■ Example: CHAIRPERSON heads DEPARTMENT. 
■ One-to-Many (1:N): An entity in A is associated with any number of entities in B, but an entity in B is associated with at most one in A. 
■ Example: MOTHER has CHILDREN. 
■Many-to-Many (M:N): Entities in both sets can be associated with any number of entities in the other. 
■ Example: STUDENT attends CLASS. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
41/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Structural Constraints & Logic 
Participation Constraints (Min Cardinality) 
■Total Participation (Mandatory): Every entity in the set must participate in at least one relationship instance. 
■ Existential Dependency: The entity cannot exist in the database without its 
partner. 
■Notation: Double line in Chen notation. 
■ Example: Every LOAN must be associated with at least one CUSTOMER. 
■ Partial Participation (Optional): Some entities in the set may not participate in the relationship. 
■Notation: Single line in Chen notation. 
■ Example: An EMPLOYEE may or may not manage a DEPARTMENT. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
42/1260 
(Min, Max) Notation 
Conceptual Modeling Structural Constraints & Logic 
■ Definition: A more formal, mathematical notation for constraints, where each participation is labeled with a pair (min, max). 
■The Rule: For an entity type E in relationship R, the pair (min, max) means that each entity e Є E must participate in at least min and at most max instances of R. 
■Common Pairs: 
■ (0,1): Optional, at most one (Partial, 1:1). 
■ (1, 1): Mandatory, exactly one (Total, 1:1). 
■ (0, N): Optional, many (Partial, 1:N). 
■ (1, N): Mandatory, at least one (Total, 1:N). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
43/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Structural Constraints & Logic 
Structural Logic: The (Min, Max) Advantage 
Precision in Modeling 
Traditional Crow's Foot or Chen notation often struggles to express specific numeric constraints (e.g., "A team must have between 5 and 11 players”). 
■ Standard notation: Just shows "Many." 
■ (Min, Max) notation: Explicitly states (5, 11). 
■Logic Check: 0 ≤ min ≤ max and max ≥ 1. 
■This notation maps directly to SQL CHECK constraints and application-level validation logic. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
44/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Structural Constraints & Logic 
Pathway Validation (Connection Traps) 
■Concept: A connection trap occurs when an ER model is designed such that it 
appears to support a relationship between entities, but the underlying data paths lead to ambiguity or loss of information. 
■The Root Cause: Misunderstanding the meaning of relationships or misplacing them 
in the hierarchy. 
■Two Primary Types: 
1. The Fan Trap 2. The Chasm Trap 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
45/1260 
The Fan Trap 
Conceptual Modeling Structural Constraints & Logic 
■ Definition: Ambiguity arises when two 1:N relationships "fan out" from a single entity, making it impossible to determine which leaf entities are associated with each other. 
■Structure: B← A→ C 
■ Example: DIVISION operates many BRANCHES; DIVISION employs many STAFF. 
■The Trap: If we know Staff Member X works for Division Y, and Division Y has Branches 1 and 2, we cannot logically determine which Branch Staff Member X is stationed at. 
■The Solution: Restructure to DIVISION → BRANCH → STAFF.

■The Scenario: A Division operates multiple Branches and employs many Staff.

2025/26/2 
47/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Structural Constraints & Logic 
The Fan Trap: An Example (Ambiguity) II 
The Logical Failure 
We know Staff X and Staff Y work for Division 1. 
■ We know Division 1 operates Branch A and Branch B. 
■The Trap: To which specific Branch is Staff X assigned? The model provides no pathway to answer this. The "fan" out from Division creates ambiguity. 
Resolution: Re-order the pathway: Division → Branch → Staff. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
48/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Structural Constraints & Logic 
Fan Trap Fix: The Schema 
■Schema: DEPARTMENT (1)→ (N) PROFESSOR and DEPARTMENT (1) → (N) STUDENT. 
■ Visual: 
Professor ← Department → Student 
■The Question: "Which professors advise which students?" 
■The Trap: We know Prof. Green and Student Alice are in the History Dept. We cannot conclude Green advises Alice. The path through "Department" is too broad. 
■Data Result: A join would produce a Cartesian Product of all Professors and Students in that Dept-mostly false associations. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
49/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Structural Constraints & Logic 
Fan Trap Fix: Re-linking the Path 
■Logic: We must provide a semantic link between the "leaves” of the fan. 
■ Corrected Schema: DEPARTMENT → PROFESSOR → STUDENT. 
■ Validation: 
1. We know Student Alice is advised by Prof. Green. 
2. We know Prof. Green works for the History Dept. 
3. Therefore, we can infer Alice is in the History Dept correctly. 
■The path is now a deterministic chain rather than an ambiguous fan.

STAFF 
Definition: Ambiguity arises when two 1:N relationships "fan out" from a central entity. 
The Problem: We know Staff works for Division, and Division has Branches. We lose the link between specific Staff and specific Branch. 
The Fix: Restructure to linear path: Div→ Branch → Staff.

2025/26/2 
51/1260 
The Chasm Trap 
Conceptual Modeling Structural Constraints & Logic 
■ Definition: A loss of logical pathways occurs due to "partial participation" (optionality) in a chain of relationships. 
■ Structure: A→B→ C (where the link to C is optional). 
■ Example: BRANCH has STAFF; STAFF oversees PROPERTY. 
■The Trap: If some Properties are not yet assigned to a Staff member (Optional Participation), we lose the logical link between that Property and its parent Branch. 
■The Solution: Add a direct (redundant but necessary) relationship between BRANCH and PROPERTY to ensure path continuity.

The Chasm Trap: An Example (Lost Pathways) | 
■The Scenario: A Branch has many Staff, and Staff oversee Properties (Optional).

2025/26/2 
53/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Structural Constraints & Logic 
The Chasm Trap: An Example (Lost Pathways) II 
The Logical Failure 
■ Property 101 and 102 are linked to Branch Alpha via their assigned Staff. 
■ The Trap: Property 103 is currently unmanaged (Staff is NULL). 
■ Because the relationship between Staff and Property is optional (min = 0), the path from Property 103 back to Branch Alpha is broken. We have lost the knowledge of which Branch owns the property. 
Resolution: Add a direct, mandatory relationship: Branch → Property. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
54/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Structural Constraints & Logic 
Chasm Trap Fix: The Schema 
■ Schema: BRANCH (1)→ (N) STAFF (0) → (N) PROPERTY. 
■ Note: Participation of PROPERTY in "Staff-Oversees-Property" is Partial (0). 
■The Question: "Which branch is responsible for Property #101?" 
■The Trap: If Property #101 is new and hasn't been assigned a Staff member yet, the link to the Branch is broken. 
■ The database suggests the property belongs to no branch, even though every property is owned by the firm. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
55/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Structural Constraints & Logic 
Chasm Trap Fix: Strategic Redundancy 
■The Rule: When a path is broken by optionality, we must bridge the chasm. 
■ Solution: Create a direct relationship: BRANCH lists PROPERTY. 
■Logic: 
■ Property → Branch (Mandatory: Every property must belong to a branch). 
■ Property → Staff (Optional: May not have an agent yet). 
■We now have a guaranteed path from every property to its branch.

60/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Structural Constraints & Logic 
Recursive Constraints: Symmetry 
■ Symmetric Recursion: Relationship Married_To on Person. 
■If A is married to B, then B is married to A. 
■Cardinality: (0,1). 
■Constraint: XY (Cannot marry self). 
Design Challenge 
Standard ER does not distinguish between (A, B) and (B, A) in the instance set. In SQL, this often leads to storing double rows or forcing a lexicographical order (ID_1 < ID_2) to store unique pairs.

61/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Structural Constraints & Logic 
Recursive Constraints: Asymmetry 
■ Asymmetric Recursion: Relationship Manages on Employee. 
■Roles: Supervisor (0, N) and Subordinate (1, 1). 
■ Logic: 
■ A Supervisor can manage many. 
■ A Subordinate has exactly one Supervisor. 
■ Hierarchy Constraint: The graph must be a tree (or forest). No cycles allowed 
(A → B → A). This is a semantic constraint rarely enforceable by diagram syntax alone.

62/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Structural Constraints & Logic 
Ternary Cardinality: The Look-Across Rule 
■ How do we interpret a cardinality of "1" in a ternary relationship (A, B, C)? 
■Rule: Fix two entities and look at the third. 
■ Example: Supply(Supplier, Part, Project). 
■If Part has cardinality 1: 
■ For a given (Supplier, Project) pair, there can be at most one Part. 
■Functional Dependency: {Supplier, Project} → Part. 
This logic is critical for determining the Primary Key of the resulting relational table.

63/1260 
Limitations of Basic ER 
Conceptual Modeling Enhanced ER (EER) & Knowledge Representation 
■The Semantic Gap: Basic ER is excellent for standard entities but struggles with complex relationships, taxonomies, and shared properties. 
■Motivation for EER: 
■To model subclasses and superclasses. 
■To support attribute and relationship inheritance. 
■To represent "IS-A" hierarchies (e.g., a Manager is a specific kind of Employee). 
■ Knowledge Representation: EER moves the database model closer to Object-Oriented paradigms and Ontological structures.

65/1260 
EER Example 
Conceptual Modeling Enhanced ER (EER) & Knowledge Representation 
VIN 
VEHICLE

OWNER 
CAR 
TRUCK 
NumDoors

PERSON 
COMPANY 
Logic Breakdown 
1. Total Disjoint (d + double line): A VEHICLE must be either a CAR or a TRUCK. It cannot be a generic vehicle, and it cannot 
be both. 
2. Inheritance: Both CAR and TRUCK inherit the VIN from the superclass. 
3. Category (U): An OWNER is a "Union Type." An owner instance is either a PERSON or a COMPANY. This is used because 
Persons and Companies have completely different attributes (e.g., SSN vs. TaxID).

2025/26/2 
66/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Enhanced ER (EER) & Knowledge Representation 
Superclasses and Subclasses 
■ Superclass: A generic entity type that contains the common properties of several subgroupings (e.g., EMPLOYEE). 
■ Subclass: A subgrouping of entities that possesses meaningful distinct properties (e.g., SECRETARY, ENGINEER). 
■The IS-A Relationship: An entity in a subclass is also an entity in the superclass. 
■Type Inheritance: A subclass entity inherits all attributes of the superclass and all relationships in which the superclass participates. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
67/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Enhanced ER (EER) & Knowledge Representation 
Specialization vs. Generalization 
■ Specialization (Top-Down): The process of defining a set of subclasses of an entity type. 
■Logic: Starting with an entity and identifying specific variants based on distinguishing characteristics. 
■ Generalization (Bottom-Up): The process of identifying common features among several entity types and "extracting" them into a single superclass. 
■Logic: Starting with multiple entities and suppressing their differences to find a common denominator. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
68/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Enhanced ER (EER) & Knowledge Representation 
Constraints on Specialization: Disjointness 
■Definition: Specifies whether an instance of a superclass can belong to more than one subclass simultaneously. 
■ Disjoint Constraint (d): An entity can be a member of at most one subclass. 
■ Example: A PART is either MANUFACTURED or PURCHASED; it cannot be both. 
■ Overlapping Constraint (o): An entity can be a member of multiple subclasses simultaneously. 
■ Example: A PERSON at a university can be both a STUDENT and an EMPLOYEE. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
69/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Enhanced ER (EER) & Knowledge Representation 
Constraints on Specialization: Completeness 
■ Definition: Specifies whether every entity in the superclass must belong to at least one subclass. 
■Total Completeness (Double Line): Every superclass entity must be a member of at least one subclass. 
■ Example: Every STUDENT must be either a GRADUATE or UNDERGRADUATE. 
■ Partial Completeness (Single Line): An entity may exist purely as a member of the superclass without belonging to any subclass. 
■ Example: An EMPLOYEE might not be a MANAGER or an ENGINEER; they could just be a generic employee. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
70/1260 
The Constraint Matrix 
Conceptual Modeling Enhanced ER (EER) & Knowledge Representation 
Possible Combinations 
1. Disjoint + Total: Mandatory membership in exactly one subclass. 
2. Disjoint + Partial: Optional membership in at most one subclass. 
3. Overlapping + Total: Mandatory membership in at least one subclass (potentially 
many). 
4. Overlapping + Partial: Completely optional membership in any number of subclasses. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
71/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Enhanced ER (EER) & Knowledge Representation 
Specialization Lattice (Multiple Inheritance) 
■ Scenario: University Roles. 
■Superclass: Person. 
■ Subclasses: Employee, Student. 
■ Shared Subclass: Teaching Assistant (TA). 
■Logic: A TA is a Employee AND is a Student. 
■ Inheritance: The TA entity inherits attributes from both paths (Salary from Emp, GPA from Student). 
■ Implementation Risk: The "Diamond Problem" in mapping strategies (duplication of keys). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
72/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Enhanced ER (EER) & Knowledge Representation 
Union Types (Categories) 
■The Problem: Standard specialization allows one superclass to have multiple subclasses. What if a subclass has multiple different superclasses? 
■ Definition: A subclass that represents a collection of entities that is a subset of the union of entities from different entity types. 
■ Example: An OWNER of a vehicle can be a PERSON, a BANK, or a COMPANY. 
■ Difference from Multiple Inheritance: 
■ Multiple Inheritance: An entity must be a member of all superclasses. 
■ Union Type: An entity is a member of exactly one of the participating superclasses. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
73/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Enhanced ER (EER) & Knowledge Representation 
Union Types (Categories): Example 
■ Scenario: A Vehicle can be owned by a Person, a Bank (Leased), or a Company. 
■Standard ER Failure: Creating a single superclass LegalEntity works only if they share attributes. If they are totally different, we need a Union. 
■ Structure: 
Person U Bank U Company → Owner (Category) 
■Relationship: Owner owns Vehicle. 
■Constraint: An instance of Owner is an instance of exactly one of the superclasses. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
74/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Enhanced ER (EER) & Knowledge Representation 
Aggregation 
■Concept: A limitation of basic ER is that relationships cannot participate in other relationships. 
■ Definition: Treating a relationship (and its associated entities) as a higher-level aggregate entity. 
■Usage: When we need to express a relationship between a relationship and another entity. 
■ Example: 
■ Entities: EMPLOYEE, PROJECT. 
■Relationship: WORKS_ON. 
■ Requirement: We need to track which MANAGER authorizes an employee to work on a specific project. 
■ Aggregation: The WORKS ON link is "boxed" and treated as an entity that connects to MANAGER via the AUTHORIZES relationship. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
75/1260 
Bridging ER and UML 
Conceptual Modeling Comparative Conceptual Syntax 
■ER Modeling: Traditionally used by database architects for data-at-rest; focused on storage and integrity. 
■ UML Class Diagrams: Used by software engineers for data-in-motion; focused on object state and behavior. 
■ The Convergence: Modern system design requires translating between these notations to ensure the application logic matches the database schema. 
■ Unified Modeling Language (UML): Provides a standardized way to visualize system design, where "Classes" act as the functional equivalent of "Entities."

78/1260 
Entities vs. Classes 
Conceptual Modeling Comparative Conceptual Syntax 
■Entity (ER): A passive data container. Focus is strictly on the attributes that define the state. 
■ Class (UML): A blueprint that includes both Attributes (State) and Operations 
(Behavior). 
■The Mapping: 
■ When mapping a UML Class to a Relational Entity, the operations (methods) are generally discarded or moved to the Application Layer / Stored Procedures. 
■ Private (-) vs. Public (+) visibility in UML has no direct equivalent in standard ER, though it informs Database Security views. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
79/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Comparative Conceptual Syntax 
Associations vs. Relationships 
■ER Notation: Relationships are often represented by diamonds (Chen) or lines with specific "Crow's Foot" endings. 
■ UML Notation: Associations are simple lines between classes. 
■ Association Classes: The UML equivalent of an Associative Entity or a relationship with attributes. 
■ Navigability: UML can specify the direction of a relationship (arrowheads), whereas standard ER relationships are bidirectional by default. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
80/1260 
Conceptual Modeling Comparative Conceptual Syntax 
Terminology: Cardinality vs. Multiplicity 
■ Cardinality (ER Modeling): 
■ Technically refers only to the Maximum number of entities in a relationship (the "Cardinality Ratio"). 
■ Values: 1:1, 1:N, M:N. 
■Companion Concept: "Participation" defines the minimum (0 or 1). 
■ Multiplicity (UML Modeling): 
■ A single term that combines both Minimum and Maximum instances. 
■ Notation: min..max (e.g., 0..1, 1..*, 5..5). 
■It is a property of an "Association End." 
The Practical Difference 
In ER, we usually talk about Cardinality and Participation separately. In UML, we wrap them together into a single concept called Multiplicity.

81/1260 
Logic as a Foundation 
Topic 2 
Logic as a Foundation First-Order Logic: The Foundation 
Logic as the Fondation of Databases 
■ Transition: While ER models provide a visual "map," First-Order Logic (FOL) provides 
the formal mathematical language to reason about data. 
Declarative Nature: Logic tells us what is true, not how to find it. 
■Consistency: Logic allows us to prove that a database state is valid according to the rules defined in the schema. 
Query Power: Every database query is essentially a formula in logic looking for values that make the formula "True." 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
87/1260 
Logic as a Foundation First-Order Logic: The Foundation 
The Syntax: Constants and Variables 
■ Constants: Represent specific, fixed entities in our domain of discourse. 
■ Notation: Usually lowercase letters from the start of the alphabet (a, b, c, ...) or names like alice, bob, db_course. 
Database Equivalent: A specific value in a cell. 
■ Variables: Placeholders for unknown or arbitrary entities. 
■ Notation: Usually lowercase letters from the end of the alphabet (x, y, z, ...). 
■ Database Equivalent: A parameter in a query or a column identifier. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
88/1260 
The Syntax: Predicates 
Logic as a Foundation First-Order Logic: The Foundation 
■ Predicates: Define properties of entities or relationships between them. 
■ Arity: The number of arguments a predicate takes. 
■ Unary (Arity 1): Describes a property. 
Example: Student(x) (True if x is a student). 
■Binary (Arity 2): Describes a binary relationship. Example: Enrolled(x, y) (True if x is enrolled in y). 
■N-ary: General associations between n entities. 
Database Equivalent: A table name is a predicate; a row is a "fact" where that predicate holds true. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2

90/1260 
Logic as a Foundation First-Order Logic: The Foundation 
Quantifiers: Universal (V) 
■ Universal Quantifier (V): Used to express that a property holds for all members of the domain. 
■Syntax: VxP(x) reads as "For every x, P(x) is true." 
Example: "Every student must have an ID." 
■ Vx(Student(x) → HasID(x)) 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
91/1260 
Logic as a Foundation First-Order Logic: The Foundation 
Quantifiers: Existential (3) 
Existential Quantifier (3): Used to express that at least one member of the domain satisfies a property. 
■Syntax: 3xP(x) reads as "There exists an x such that P(x) is true." 
■ Example: "There is at least one course with no prerequisites.” 
■ 3x(Course(x) A¬3yPrereq(y, x)) 
■Relational Constraint: This is the foundation of "participation constraints" and EXISTS queries in SQL.

2025/26/2 
92/1260 
Logic as a Foundation 
First-Order Logic: The Foundation 
Vacuous Truth in Universal Quantification (V) 
■Logical Form: Vx(P(x) → Q(x)). 
The "Vacuous Truth" Problem: What if there are no x such that P(x) is true? 
"All employees in the Mars Branch earn $1M." 
If the Mars Branch is empty, the statement is true in classical logic. 
■ Database Impact: A constraint enforcing "All items in a shipment must have a weight assigned" is satisfied by an empty shipment.

93/1260 
Logic as a Foundation 
First-Order Logic: The Foundation 
Translating English to Logic: Universal Rules 
■ Scenario: "All Managers must be Employees." 
■ Correct Translation: Vx(Manager(x) → Employee (x)) 
■The Implication Logic: "For any entity x, IF x is a Manager, THEN x is an Employee." If x is not a Manager, the statement is trivially true. 
Using Conjunction (^) with V 
Vx(Manager(x) Ʌ Employee(x)) 
"Everything in the universe is both a Manager and an Employee."

94/1260 
Logic as a Foundation First-Order Logic: The Foundation 
Translating English to Logic: Existential Facts 
Scenario: "Some Employees are assigned to a Project." 
■ Correct Translation: 3x(Employee (x) A Assigned(x)) 
■The Conjunction Logic: "There exists at least one x such that x is an Employee AND x is Assigned." 
Using Implication (→) with 3 
3x(Employee(x) → Assigned(x)) 
This is true if there is anything in the universe that is not an Employee (vacuously true). It fails to assert the existence of an assigned employee.

95/1260 
Logic as a Foundation First-Order Logic: The Foundation 
Quantifier Scope: The Order Matters 
Statement A: "Every student has an advisor." 
■ Vs (Student(s) → a(Advisor (a) A Advises (a, s))) 
■Logic: For each student, pick a (potentially unique) advisor. 
■Statement B: "There is one advisor who advises every student." 
■ 3a (Advisor(a) A VS (Student(s) → Advises (a, s))) 
■Logic: A single "Super-Advisor" exists for the whole population. 
■DB Impact: Statement A allows 1:N or M:N. Statement B implies a specific structural bottleneck.

2025/26/2 
96/1260 
Negation and Scope 
Logic as a Foundation First-Order Logic: The Foundation 
■Scenario: "Not all projects are successful." 
■Translation 1: Vx(Project(x)→ Successful (x)) 
■Translation 2 (Equivalent): 3x (Project(x) A¬Successful(x)) 
■Meaning: "There exists at least one project that is not successful." 
■Constraint Logic: To enforce "All projects must be successful," the database checks if Translation 2 returns any rows. If it does, the constraint is violated.

97/1260 
Logic as a Foundation First-Order Logic: The Foundation 
Constraint Modeling: The "Only" Clause 
■ Scenario: "Only Professors can teach Courses." 
■Logical Reformulation: "If x teaches a Course, then x must be a Professor." 
■ Translation: Vx(3y(Course(y) ▲ Teaches(x, y)) → Professor(x)) 
■ Database Check: Find any x who teaches y where x is NOT a Professor.

98/1260 
Logic as a Foundation First-Order Logic: The Foundation 
Model Theory: Interpretations 
■ Model Theory: The study of the relationship between logical formulas and the structures that make them true. 
■ Interpretation (I): A mapping that gives meaning to the symbols. 
1. Domain (D): A non-empty set of objects (the "Universe" of the database). 2. Mapping Constants: Each constant c is assigned an object c1E D. 
3. Mapping Predicates: Each n-ary predicate P is assigned a set of n-tuples 
pl ≤ Dn. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
99/1260 
Satisfaction (F) 
Logic as a Foundation First-Order Logic: The Foundation 
■ Satisfaction: We say an interpretation I satisfies a formula (written IF) if the formula is true under that interpretation. 
■The Model: If I, then I is a Model for . 
■ Database Perspective: 
■The Database Instance is the Interpretation (I). 
The Integrity Constraints are the Formulas (p). 
■ A database instance is "valid" only if it is a Model for its constraints. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
100/1260 
Logic as a Foundation First-Order Logic: The Foundation 
Logical Implication and Querying 
■Logical Implication: means every interpretation that satisfies also satisfies ψ. 
■ Queries as Formulas: 
■ A query is a formula (x) with free variables. 
■The Result Set is the set of all constants c such that the database I satisfies the formula when x is replaced by c: 
{c | I = $(c)} 
■This formalizes the idea that querying is "proving" which data points satisfy a logical condition.

■ Domain D: {Alice, Bob}

"Every student has a grade.”

■ Student (Alice) is True.

■Result: True. 
Check x = Bob: 
■ Student (Bob) is False. 
■ Implication (F → ...) is trivially True. 
■ Conclusion: IF (The database is valid).

104/1260 
Logic as a Foundation Prolog: The Logic Programming Environment 
Prolog and the Knowledge Base Paradigm 
■Transition: If first-order logic is the language of design, Prolog is the first practical environment for executing that logic. 
■The Paradigm Shift: In traditional programming, you tell the computer how to solve a problem. In Prolog, you provide a Knowledge Base (KB) and ask the computer what is true. 
■ The Knowledge Base Structure: 
1. Facts: Ground truths about the world. 
2. Rules: Logical implications that allow the derivation of new truths from existing 
facts. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
105/1260 
Logic as a Foundation Prolog: The Logic Programming Environment 
Prolog in Action: Defining the Database 
%--- Facts (The Extensional Database) --- 
parent(bob, alice). 
parent(bob, david). 
parent(alice, charlie). 
%--- Rules (The Intensional Database) 
% X is a grandparent of Z IF X is parent of Y AND Y is parent of Z grandparent(X, Z):- parent(X, Y), parent(Y, Z). 
% X is a sibling of Y IF they share a parent Z AND X is not Y sibling(X, Y) :- parent(Z, X), parent(Z, Y), X \= Y. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
106/1260 
Logic as a Foundation Prolog: The Logic Programming Environment 
Prolog in Action: Performing Queries 
?- grandparent(bob, charlie). 
true. 
?- sibling(alice, Who). 
Who = david. 
■ Constants: bob, alice (lowercase). 
■ Variables: X, Y, Who (Uppercase or starts with _). 
■ Operators: - means "IF";, means "AND".

107/1260 
Logic as a Foundation Prolog: The Logic Programming Environment 
Prolog in Action: The Differentiation Program 
The following code implements a symbolic differentiator. 
■ Logic: It recursively breaks down the expression tree using the rules of calculus. 
Example (A Symbolic Differentiator) 
% Base cases 
d(X, X, 1) :- !. 
d(C,_, 0):- number(C), !. 
d(C,, 0):- atom(C), !. 
% Linearity 
% d/dx (x) = 1 
% d/dx (constant) = 0 % d/dx (y) = 0 (other var) 
d(U + V, X, DU + DV):- d(U, X, DU), d(V, X, DV). 
d(U - V, X, DU - DV) :- d(U, X, DU), d(V, X, DV). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
108/1260 
Logic as a Foundation Prolog: The Logic Programming Environment 
Prolog in Action: The Differentiation Program 
■The following code implements a symbolic differentiator. 
■Logic: It recursively breaks down the expression tree using the rules of calculus. 
Example (A Symbolic Differentiator) 
% Product and Quotient Rules 
d(U * V, X, U * DV + V * DU) :- d(U, X, DU), d(V, X, DV). 
d(U / V, X, (DU * V - U * DV) / (V * V)) :- d(U, X, DU), d(V, X, DV). 
% Power Rule 
d(U ^ N, X, N * U ^ N1 * DU) :- integer(N), N1 is N - 1, d(U, X, DU). % Chain Rule for Transcendental Functions 
d(sin(U), X, cos(U)* DU) :- d(U, X, DU). d(exp(U), X, exp(U) * DU) :- d(U, X, DU). d(log(U), X, (1 / U) * DU) :- d(U, X, DU). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
108/1260 
Logic as a Foundation Prolog: The Logic Programming Environment 
Differentiator: Query and Analysis 
■Complex Query: Find the derivative of x2. sin(x). 
Example (Symbolic Execution) 
?- d(x^2 * sin(x), x, Result). 
Result = (x^2)* (cos(x) * 1) + sin(x) * (2 * x^1 * 1). 
■ Why this is impressive: 
1. No Parser needed: Prolog's operator precedence automatically reads x^2 * 
sin(x) as a tree. 
2. Pattern Matching: The engine "sees" that the top operator is* and automatically 
triggers the Product Rule. 
3. Symbolic Logic: The result is not a number, but a new logical term that can be 
further processed (e.g., simplified or converted to code). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
109/1260 
Logic as a Foundation Prolog: The Logic Programming Environment 
Next Level: Simplification 
■ Symbolic results often contain "noise" like 1 * X or X + 0. 
We can write a second program to simplify the result by applying algebraic axioms. 
Example (Algebraic Simplifier) 
s(X + 0, X):- !. 
s(0 + X, X):- !. 
s(x * 1, X) :- !. 
s(1 * X, X):- !. 
s(x * 0, 0) :- !. 
s(A + B, NewA + NewB) :- s(A, NewA), s(B, NewB). 
s(A * B, NewA * NewB) :- s(A, NewA), s(B, NewB). 
s(X, X). % Fallback 
■By combining d/3 and s/2, we build a computer algebra system (CAS) using the same logical principles that drive database query optimization.

Logic as a Foundation Prolog: The Logic Programming Environment 
Facts: The Extensional Database (EDB) 
■Definition: Facts are predicates applied to constants (Ground Atoms). 
Database Mapping: In database theory, these facts are called the Extensional Database, representing the physical data stored. 
■ Syntax Example: 
■ parent(bob, alice). → "Bob is a parent of Alice." 
■ parent(alice, charlie). → "Alice is a parent of Charlie." 
■ Note: Facts always end with a period (.) in Prolog.

111/1260 
Rules and Horn Clauses 
Logic as a Foundation Prolog: The Logic Programming Environment 
■ Definition: Rules allow us to define complex relationships. In Prolog, these are represented as Horn Clauses. 
■Structure: Head - Body. 
■Logical Meaning: Body 
Head (If the body is true, then the head is true). 
■ The Intensional Database (IDB): These rules act as Views-virtual relations that are not stored physically but are computed as needed. 
■ Example: 
■ grandparent(X, Z) :- parent(X, Y), parent(Y, Z). 
■"X is a grandparent of Z if X is a parent of Y and Y is a parent of Z.”

Logic as a Foundation Prolog: The Logic Programming Environment 
Querying the Environment 
The Query Prompt: Once the KB is loaded, we interact via queries. 
■ 1. Verification Queries (Ground): 
■ ?- parent(bob, alice). 
■ Result: true. or false. 
■ 2. Search Queries (Non-Ground): 
■ ?- parent(bob, X). 
■ Prolog attempts to find all Variable Substitutions for X that satisfy the KB. 
■ Result: X = alice.

113/1260 
Logic as a Foundation Prolog: The Logic Programming Environment 
Search Mechanics: Unification 
■ Unification: The core "Matching" algorithm. It is often described as a "Smart Join." 
■Goal: To make two logical expressions identical by finding compatible substitutions for their variables. 
■Rules of Unification: 
1. A constant unifies only with itself. 
2. An uninstantiated variable unifies with anything (and becomes that value). 3. Complex expressions unify if their predicates match and their arguments unify. Relational Connection: This is the theoretical basis for matching Foreign Keys to Primary Keys.

parent? Yes.

■Compare Arity: 2 == 2? Yes. 
■Frame 2: Argument 1

■ Action: Bind {Y \ alice}.

■ Successful Unification.

Unification Failure: Conflict 
Goal: Unify teaches(prolog, X) with teaches(java, bob). 
■Step 1: Compare teaches == teaches. Yes. 
■Step 2: Compare prolog (Constant) with java (Constant). 
■Conflict: Two different constants cannot unify. 
■Result: Unification Fails. 
■ DB Equivalent: A join condition A.id = B.id where 101 != 102.

117/1260 
Logic as a Foundation Prolog: The Logic Programming Environment 
Search Mechanics: Backtracking 
■ Algorithm: Prolog uses SLD-Resolution with a Depth-First Search strategy. 
The Process: 
1. Prolog attempts to satisfy the first goal in a query. 
2. If multiple rules/facts match, it creates a "Choice Point." 
3. If a path fails (leads to a contradiction), Prolog backtracks to the last choice point 
and tries the next alternative. 
■ Exhaustive Search: This allows Prolog to find all possible answers to a query. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
118/1260 
Logic as a Foundation Prolog: The Logic Programming Environment 
Querying as Theorem Proving 
■The Paradigm Shift: In a usual database, we "search" for data. In Prolog, we ask the system to prove that a statement is a logical consequence of the KB. 
■The Components of a Proof: 
■ Axioms: The Facts and Rules in the Knowledge Base. 
■ Conjecture (Theorem): The Query submitted by the user. 
■ Proof: The sequence of steps (inference) connecting axioms to the conjecture. 
The Result: 
true: The conjecture can be proven (it is a theorem). 
■false: The conjecture cannot be proven. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
119/1260 
Proof by Refutation 
Logic as a Foundation Prolog: The Logic Programming Environment 
■ Prolog uses Reductio ad Absurdum (Proof by Contradiction). 
■The Procedure: 
1. Take the Query (Goal) and negate it. 
2. Add this negated goal to the set of axioms in the KB. 
3. Try to derive a Contradiction (represented as the empty clause ). 
If the system finds a contradiction, it means the negated goal is impossible, therefore the original goal must be true. 
■ Variable Binding: If the query has variables, the proof procedure finds the specific values (substitutions) that make the proof hold. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
120/1260 
Logic as a Foundation Prolog: The Logic Programming Environment 
Formal Theory: Entailment and Proof 
Let K be a Knowledge Base (a set of First-Order Logic formulas). 
■Let Q be a Query (a formula we wish to check). 
■Logical Entailment (K F Q): Q is true in every interpretation (model) that makes K true. 
■ Provability (K + Q): There exists a sequence of formal steps (a proof) starting from K that ends at Q. 
Completeness and Soundness 
For the logic used in Prolog (Horn Clauses), our proof procedure is: 
■Sound: If we prove it, it is logically true (KQ ⇒ K = Q). 
■Complete: If it is logically true, we can prove it (KF Q⇒ K + Q).

121/1260 
Logic as a Foundation Prolog: The Logic Programming Environment 
Formalizing the Horn Clause 
■ A Horn Clause is a disjunction of literals with at most one positive literal. 
■ Definite Clause (Rule): (A V ¬B1 V ¬B2 ... V¬Вn) 
Logically equivalent to: (B1 ^ B2 ... ^ Bn) → A 
■ Prolog Syntax: A:- B1, B2, ..., Bn. 
■ Negative Clause (Goal/Query): (¬G1 V ¬G2 ... V ¬Gn) 
■This represents the negated query ¬(G1 A... Ʌ Gn). 
■ Prolog Syntax: ?- G1, G2, ..., Gn.

122/1260 
Logic as a Foundation Prolog: The Logic Programming Environment 
The Resolution Inference Rule 
■Resolution is the single rule of inference used to find contradictions. 
■The Rule: Given two clauses (PVA) and (¬P V B), we can derive (A V B). 
■In Prolog, we resolve a Goal with a Rule: 
Example (Resolution Step) 
1. Goal: parent(bob, X) V ¬male(X) 
2. Fact: parent(bob, alice) 
3. Resolvent: ¬male(alice) 
The positive parent atom and negative parent atom cancel each other out.

Logic as a Foundation Prolog: The Logic Programming Environment 
Existential Proofs and Witnesses 
■ When a query contains a variable X, the user is asking: 
K = FX Q(X) 
■The Witness: To prove an existential statement, the system must find a specific constant c such that Q(c) is true. 
■The Substitution (0): This is the mapping of variables to terms (e.g., {X \ alice}). 
■ The "Answer" provided by Prolog is technically the Correct Answer Substitution: the set of bindings that allowed the resolution to reach the empty clause.

124/1260 
Logic as a Foundation 
Prolog: The Logic Programming Environment 
SLD-Resolution: The Proof Method 
■SLD stands for Selective Linear Definite clause resolution. 
■ How it works (Goal Reduction): 
1. Start with a list of goals (the query). 
2. Pick the first goal and find a rule whose Head matches (unifies). 
3. Replace that goal with the Body of the rule. 
4. If the goal list becomes empty, the proof is complete. 
Example (Proof Trace) 
?- grandparent(bob, Who). 
→ Apply Rule: parent(bob, Y), parent(Y, Who). 
→ Match Fact: parent(bob, alice). (Bind Y = alice) 
→ New Goal: parent(alice, Who). 
→ Match Fact: parent(alice, charlie). (Bind Who = charlie) 
→ Goal Empty: Proof complete.

goal.

■ Unification (01): {X \ bob, Z \ Who}

■ Unification (03): {Who \ charlie} 
The Empty Clause (□) 
The last negative literal is canceled out. There are no goals left to prove. We have derived a contradiction between the negated query and the KB. 
Result: The query is True. 
Composition of Substitutions: 01°02° 03 = {Who = charlie}.

Logic as a Foundation Prolog: The Logic Programming Environment 
Prolog as a General Language: Recursion 
■ Recursive Logic: Prolog does not use loops (for, while). It uses recursion to process repetitive structures. 
■Example: Transitive Closure 
■ ancestor(X, Y):- parent(X, Y). (Base Case) 
■ ancestor(X, Y) :- parent(X, Z), ancestor(Z, Y). (Recursive Step) 
■ Power: Prolog is a general-purpose programming language. You can compute anything in Prolog that you can compute in other languages.

Logic as a Foundation 
Prolog: The Logic Programming Environment 
Data Structures: Lists and Functors 
■Functors: Complex terms created by function symbols. 
■Lists: The fundamental structure in Prolog. 
■ Syntax: [Head | Tail]. 
■ Internal Representation: cons(Head, Tail). 
■ Unification Example: 
■Goal: Unify [a, b, c] with [X | Y]. 
■Result: X = a, Y = [b, c]. 
This allows processing sequences recursively (Head first, then recurse on Tail).

131/1260 
Logic as a Foundation Prolog: The Logic Programming Environment 
Trap 1: The Procedural Loop (Left-Recursion) 
■The Issue: The answer exists and is finite, but the search engine never finds it because of rule ordering. 
Life Analogy: You are looking for a specific book. You follow a sign that says "To find books, go to the next floor" before you look at the shelf in front of you. You will climb stairs forever, even if the book was on the ground floor. 
The Left-Recursive Path 
% This will loop infinitely on the first goal ancestor(X, Y) :- ancestor(X, Z), parent(Z, Y). ancestor(X, Y) :- parent(X, Y). 
?- ancestor(bob, charlie). % Infinite Loop! 
■Reason: Prolog is Top-Down and Left-to-Right. It tries to solve the first goal of the first rule. To solve ancestor, it tries to solve ancestor... 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
132/1260 
Logic as a Foundation Prolog: The Logic Programming Environment 
Trap 2: The Generative Trap (Infinite Answer Set) 
The Issue: The logic is sound, but the set of correct answers is mathematically infinite. 
The search doesn't terminate because there is always "one more" answer. 
The Successor Function 
is_number(0). 
is_number(s(X)) :- is_number(X). % s(X) means "Successor of X" 
?- is_number(X). 
X = 0; 
X = s(0); 
X = s(s(0)); 
(Infinite sequence of integers) 
Database Impact: This "answer set" can never be fully materialized in memory.

133/1260 
Introduction to Datalog 
Logic as a Foundation Datalog: The Tamed Database Subset 
■ Definition: Datalog is a declarative logic language that is a syntactic subset of Prolog, specifically designed for interaction with relational databases. 
■The Goal: To provide a logic language that is more powerful than Relational Algebra (specifically for recursion) but guarantees termination and efficiency. 
■ Key Difference from Prolog: Datalog removes features that lead to infinite loops or non-deterministic behavior, such as function symbols and specific evaluation orders. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
134/1260 
Logic as a Foundation Datalog: The Tamed Database Subset 
Restricting the Environment 
■Removal of Function Symbols: Datalog forbids function symbols (e.g., f(x)). 
■ This ensures the Herbrand Universe (the set of all possible constants) is finite. 
■ If the domain is finite, the number of facts we can derive is finite, guaranteeing query termination. 
■Safe Rules: A rule is "safe" if every variable that appears in the Head of the rule also appears in a non-negated (positive) atom in the Body. 
■ Unsafe: unassigned_staff(X):- not works_on(X, Project). (Who is X? The set could be infinite). 
■ Safe: unassigned_staff(X):- \textbf{employee(X)}, not works_on(X, __). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
135/1260 
Logic as a Foundation 
Datalog: The Tamed Database Subset 
Safety Logic: Formal Definition 
Range Restriction Property: Every variable appearing in the Head or in a Negated subgoal or in a Comparison (X > Y) must also appear in a positive, ordinary EDB/IDB subgoal in the Body. 
■Rule Safety: A rule is Safe if and only if every variable in the rule satisfies the Range Restriction Property. 
■Reason: Variables in positive body atoms are bound to finite values from the database. Variables elsewhere are potentially infinite. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
136/1260 
Logic as a Foundation 
Datalog: The Tamed Database Subset 
Safety Logic: Negation and Bound Variables 
■The Rule: Every variable in the Head must appear in a positive (non-negated) atom in the Body. 
Unsafe Rule: The Infinite Complement 
dislikes(X, pizza):- not likes(X, pizza). 
Unsafe. Who is X? X could be "The Moon" or "The number 5." The database cannot compute the set of "all things" that don't like pizza. 
Example (Safe Rule: Bound by Positive Atom) 
likes(X, pizza) :- person(X). 
Safe. X is restricted to values satisfying the person relation. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
137/1260 
Logic as a Foundation Datalog: The Tamed Database Subset 
Safety Logic: Comparisons and Arithmetic 
■The Rule: Variables used in comparisons (>, <, #) must first be bound by an ordinary EDB/IDB predicate. 
Unsafe Rule: Free Variables in Arithmetic 
greater than(X, Y) :- X > Y. 
Unsafe. There are an infinite number of pairs (X, Y) where X > Y. A database engine cannot materialize an infinite result set. 
Example (Safe Rule: Anchored Comparison) 
expensive(X):- price(X, P), P > 100. 
Safe. P is first bound to a value from the price relation; only then is the math applied. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
138/1260 
Logic as a Foundation 
Datalog: The Tamed Database Subset 
EDB vs. IDB: The Data Architecture 
■ EDB (Extensional Database): 
■The set of facts stored physically in the database. 
■These are the "ground truths." 
■IDB (Intensional Database): 
■The set of rules defining virtual relations. 
■These are the "Views" or "Logic" of the system. 
■ Predicates defined in the IDB cannot appear in the EDB. 
■ Querying: Queries can be performed over the union of EDB and IDB. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
139/1260 
Logic as a Foundation 
Datalog: The Tamed Database Subset 
Evaluation Strategy: Top-Down vs. Bottom-Up 
■Top-Down (Prolog Style): 
■Starts with the query (goal) and works backward to the facts. 
■ Uses Backward Chaining. 
■ Problem: Can lead to redundant computation or infinite loops in the presence of recursion. 
Bottom-Up (Database Style): 
■Starts with the facts and applies rules to derive all possible new facts. 
■ Uses Forward Chaining. 
This is "Set-at-a-time" processing, which is much more efficient for secondary storage (disks). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
140/1260 
Logic as a Foundation Datalog: The Tamed Database Subset 
The Tp Operator and Fixed-Point Evaluation 
■The Tp Operator: A mathematical function that takes a set of facts and produces a new set of facts by applying all IDB rules exactly once. 
The Iterative Process: 
1. Start with the EDB facts (I). 
2. Apply the rules: I1 = Tp (10) U I。- 
3. Repeat: In+1 = Tp(In) U In· 
Fixed-Point: The process stops when In+1 = In. Because the domain is finite and rules are monotonic (we only add facts), we are guaranteed to reach a fixed-point.

Logic as a Foundation 
Datalog: The Tamed Database Subset 
Recursive Power: Transitive Closure 
Datalog can express transitive closure, which many systems (including FOL) cannot do. 
Example (Reachability in a Graph) 
reachable(X, Y):- edge(X, Y). 
reachable(X, Y) :- edge(X, Z), reachable(Z, Y). 
The engine will discover all paths by iteratively expanding the reachable set until no more connections can be found.

147/1260 
Logic as a Foundation Datalog: The Tamed Database Subset 
The Paradox of Recursive Negation 
■The Problem: Negation combined with recursion can lead to logical contradictions where no fixed-point exists. 
■Example: shaves (barber, X):- man(X), not shaves(X, X). 
Does the barber shave himself? 
If he does, the rule says he doesn't. If he doesn't, the rule says he does. Non-monotonicity: Negation makes the Tp operator non-monotonic (adding facts can now "delete" others), breaking the fixed-point guarantee.

148/1260 
Logic as a Foundation Datalog: The Tamed Database Subset 
The Stratification Lab: Dependency Graphs 
■To determine if a Datalog program with negation is valid, we draw the Precedence Graph. 
■Nodes: The predicates (e.g., P, Q, R). 
■ Edges: 
■ QP if rule for P uses Q positively. 
■ QP if rule for P uses Q negatively. 
■Stratifiability Condition: No cycle in the graph contains a negative edge. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
149/1260 
Logic as a Foundation Datalog: The Tamed Database Subset 
Computing Strata 
Assign a stratum number S(P) to each predicate P. 
Constraints: 
1. If Q→ P (positive), then S(P) > S(Q). 
2. If Q P (negative), then S(P) > S(Q). 
■ Valid Example: 
■Q (Base fact) → Stratum 1. 
■ PQ Stratum 2. 
■RP Stratum 2. 
We compute Stratum 1 fully before evaluating Stratum 2.

The Barber Paradox (Negative Cycle) 
Example (The Paradox Program) 
person(barber). 
shaves(barber, X):- person(X), not shaves(X, X). 
■ Graph Analysis: 
Node: shaves. 
→shaves. 
Edge: shaves → shaves. 
Cycle: A self-loop with a negative edge. 
■Result: Non-stratifiable. No fixed-point exists. 
■Database Action: The compiler rejects this query.

Mutual Recursion and Stratification 
Example (The Even/Odd Reachability) 
even(start). 
even(X):- edge(X,Y), odd(Y). 
odd(X):- edge(X,Y), even(Y). 
Graph: even → odd. 
Edges: Both are positive. 
■ Cycle: even → odd→ even. 
■Result: Stratifiable (Stratum 1 contains both). 
■Constraint: If we added odd(X):- not even(X), the cycle would include a negation, 
making it invalid.

152/1260 
Logic as a Foundation 
Datalog: The Tamed Database Subset 
Stratified Datalog 
■The Solution: Stratification. We organize rules into "layers" (strata). 
■The Rule: A predicate P can only depend on the negation of predicate Q if Q is fully computed in a lower stratum. 
■Dependency Graph: 
■ Nodes = Predicates. 
Edges = "Depends on." 
■Negative Edges = "Depends on NOT." 
■Stratifiability: A Datalog program is stratifiable if its dependency graph contains no cycles involving a negative edge. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
153/1260 
Logic as a Foundation Datalog: The Tamed Database Subset 
Perfect Model Semantics 
■ When we use Stratified Datalog, we choose a specific "Perfect Model” among many possible interpretations. 
The Goal: To find the most "natural" interpretation of recursive negation. 
■ Mechanism: 
1. Compute the facts for the lowest stratum (positive facts). 
2. Use those facts to evaluate negation in the next stratum. 
3. Continue until all strata are processed. 
■This ensures that we reach a consistent fixed-point that aligns with our human intuition of "failure to find." 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
154/1260 
Logic as a Foundation Datalog: The Tamed Database Subset 
Calculating the Perfect Model 
■ Scenario: A Stratified Program. 
■EDB: node(1), node(2), edge(1,2).. 
S1: path(X,Y):- edge(X,Y). path(X,Z):- path(X,Y), edge(Y,Z). 
■S2: unreachable(X,Y):- node(X), node(Y), not path(X,Y). 
■ Step 1 (Evaluate Stratum 1): 
Fixed Point of S1 = {path(1,2)}. 
■Step 2 (Evaluate Stratum 2): 
■Treat S1 results as EDB facts. 
■Negation Check: not path(1,2) is False. not path(2,1) is True. 
■Fixed Point of S2 includes disconnected(2,1). 
■Result: The unique, standard model preferred by the database.

155/1260 
Logic as a Foundation The Epistemology of Databases 
Epistemology: What Does the Database "Know"? 
■ Epistemology is the study of knowledge. In databases, we must define the logical relationship between the data stored and the reality it represents. 
Key Questions: 
■If a fact is not in the database, is it false or just unknown? 
■ How do we handle "negative" information without explicitly storing it? 
■ How does the database interpret a "failure to find" an answer? 
■The answers to these questions define the Semantics of the database language. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
156/1260 
Logic as a Foundation The Epistemology of Databases 
The Closed World Assumption (CWA) 
■ Formal Definition: The assumption that any statement that is not known to be true (i.e., not explicitly present or derivable) is false. 
■The DB Philosophy: The database is considered a complete representation of the domain of interest. 
■Logic: If DB 0, then we conclude ¬. 
Efficiency: This is crucial for databases because we only need to store positive facts. We don't need to store "Alice is not a pilot," "Bob is not a pilot," etc. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
157/1260 
Logic as a Foundation The Epistemology of Databases 
CWA Example: Flight Databases 
Consider a database FLIGHTS(Source, Destination). 
■Facts: (London, Paris), (Paris, Tokyo). 
■Query: Is there a flight from London to New York? 
■CWA Interpretation: Since (London, NY) is not in the database, it returns False. 
■Reality vs. Model: In the real world, the flight might exist but be missing from the DB. Under CWA, we ignore this possibility to ensure a tighter logic. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
158/1260 
Logic as a Foundation The Epistemology of Databases 
Negation as Failure (NAF) 
■ Operationalizing CWA: CWA is a theoretical assumption; Negation as Failure (NAF) is 
the implementation in languages like Prolog and Datalog. 
■The Rule: A goal not P succeeds if the attempt to prove P fails. 
■Logical Symbol: Often written as~ to distinguish it from classical negation (¬). 
■ Non-Monotonicity: NAF makes logic non-monotonic. If we add a new fact P to the database, a query for not P which previously succeeded will now fail.

Logic as a Foundation The Epistemology of Databases 
Open World Assumption (OWA) 
■Contrast: Used in the Semantic Web (RDF/OWL) and some Graph Databases (Topic 13). 
■ Definition: Failure to derive a fact does not imply it is false; it simply means it is unknown. 
■The Logic: 
If DB, then 
is True. 
■If DB, then 
is False. 
If neither, & is Unknown. 
■Use Case: Distributed systems where no single node has the "whole truth."

The Relational Model 
Topic 3 
The Relational Model Introduction to the Relational Model 
The Relational Revolution 
■ Origin: Proposed by E.F. Codd in 1970 ("A Relational Model of Data for Large Shared Data Banks"). 
■ Mathematical Shift: Moving from data structures (trees/graphs) to set theory and predicate logic. 
■ Objectives: 
High degree of data independence. 
■ Formal foundation for database semantics. 
- 
■Resolving the "pointers" problem of previous models – data was connected via physical addresses rather than logic. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
163/1260 
The Relational Model Introduction to the Relational Model 
The Model-Theoretic View: Databases as Formal Logic 
■The Shift: Instead of viewing a database as a collection of files or records, we view it as a mathematical structure governed by Finite Model Theory. 
■ Core Components: 
1. The Language (L): The signature defining the vocabulary (tables and columns). 2. The Theory (2): A set of First-Order Logic (FOL) sentences defining the rules 
(constraints). 
3. The Interpretation (3): The actual data instance that assigns truth values to the 
language. 
■Fundamental Concept: A valid database state is an interpretation 3 that is a Model of the theory Σ. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
164/1260 
The Relational Model Introduction to the Relational Model 
The Relational Signature (L): Predicate Symbols and Arity 
■The Signature (L): A countable set of relation names (predicate symbols) and their associated arity (number of arguments). 
L = {R1/n1, R2/N2, ..., Rk/nk} 
■ Predicate Symbol (R): Corresponds to the "Table Name." 
■ Arity (ni): The fixed number of attributes (columns) in the relation. 
■Function Symbols: The relational model specifically excludes function symbols (f(x)) to ensure the domain remains finite and queries remain decidable (unlike general Prolog). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
165/1260 
The Relational Model Introduction to the Relational Model 
Attributes and the Functional Mapping of Domains 
■ Attributes: A set of symbols A = {A1, ..., Am} used to name the positions in a relation. 
■ The Domain Function (dom): A mapping from an attribute name to a set of atomic 
constants. 
dom: A → P(D) 
■ Attribute-Defined Domains: 
■ We assume the domain is tied to the attribute name, not just the position (backreference: indexed families of sets in logic). 
■ If attribute A appears in relation R and relation S, then R.A and S.A share the same domain. 
■This formalism allows the Natural Join () to be defined implicitly on matching attribute names. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
166/1260 
The Relational Model Introduction to the Relational Model 
The Status of NULL: Definition and Formalism 
In formal relational theory, NULL is not a value. It is a marker or placeholder indicating the absence of data. 
A standard domain dom(A) is a set of atomic constants. NULL is, by definition, not a constant and therefore & dom(A). 
To support nullability, the relational model must utilize an extended domain. If an attribute A is nullable, its tuple positions take values from: 
dom(A) U {1} 
where represents the null state. Note that is technically a non-atomic construct, creating a theoretical tension with the "First Normal Form" requirement of atomicity. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
167/1260 
The Relational Model Introduction to the Relational Model 
The Status of NULL: Semantics and 3-Valued Logic 
A major criticism of NULL is that one marker possibly represents distinct logical states: 
State 
Unknown 
Inapplicable 
Withheld 
Semantic Meaning 
A value exists in reality, but it is missing from the DB. 
The attribute does not apply to this specific entity. 
The value is known but restricted (Security/Privacy). 
■ 3-Valued Logic (3VL): The presence of NULL markers forces a departure from 
Boolean logic (T, F). 
■Comparisons: Any comparison between a value and (or 1=1) results in the truth value Unknown. 
■Logic Impact: In the model-theoretic view, a query P(x) must now handle three possible outcomes (T, F, U), complicating the definition of Satisfaction.

r≤ dom(A1) × dom(A2) × ··· × dom(An) 
■The Tuple (t): An element t Er. Formally, a function assigning a constant cЄ dom(A) to each attribute A. 
■Set-Theoretic Properties: 
1. Unordered: {t1, t2} = {t2, t1}. 
2. Distinct: Vt1, t2 Er: (AAEU (t1[A] = t2[A]) ⇒ t1 = t2).

The Database Instance as an Herbrand Interpretation (3) 
■ Herbrand Universe (U): The set of all constants (values) appearing in the database. 
■ Herbrand Base (B): The set of all ground atoms (facts) that can be formed using predicates from £ and constants from U. 
■The Instance (3): A finite subset of the Herbrand Base. 
■ J = {R1(a, b), R1(c, d), R2 (a)} 
■Truth Value: An atom P(C1, ..., Cn) is True in J if and only if P(C1, ..., Cn) EI.

Model-Theoretic Satisfaction (J = Σ) 
Definition (Database Instance Validity) 
A database instance J is a valid state of schema Σ if and only if J satisfies every sentence in Σ. 
Υσε Σ : 1 Εσ 
■ Satisfaction (F): Determined by the standard rules of Tarski's Truth Definition. 
■Consistency: If Σ contains contradictions (e.g., A → B and A → ¬B), no valid instance J exists. 
■ DBMS Role: The database engine acts as a proof checker, rejecting any transaction A such that (UA)FE.

Formalizing Identity: Superkeys and Candidate Keys 
■Definition: Let R be a relation schema with attributes U. A subset KCU is a 
Superkey of R if, for any valid instance r(R), there are no two distinct tuples with the same values for K. 
■Logical Form: 
SK(K) Vt1, t2 Er: (t1[K] = t2[K] ⇒ t1 = t2) 
■ Candidate Key: A superkey K such that no proper subset Y cK is a superkey (minimality). 
CK(K) ⇒ SK(K) ^ ‡K' (K' — K ^ SK(K')) 
■ Primary Key: A specific candidate key selected to identify tuples.

The Relational Model 
Introduction to the Relational Model 
The Three Pillars 
■ Unique Name Assumption (UNA): Different constants denote different objects. 
■ If c1 C2 (symbolically), then cc2 (semantically). 
■ Example: 'Alice' is not 'Bob'. 
■ Domain Closure Assumption (DCA): There are no objects in the universe other than those named by the constants in the database. 
■ Enables VxP(x) to be checked by iterating over finite data. 
■ Closed World Assumption (CWA): If a fact P is not present in the instance J, it is assumed to be false. 
PEJJE¬P 
■ Allows the database to return definite "No" answers (negative information) without explicitly storing negative facts.

174/1260 
The Relational Model Relational Algebra (RA): The Procedural Language 
Introduction to Relational Algebra 
■ Definition: A theoretical query language consisting of a set of operations that take one or two relations as input and produce a new relation as output. 
■ Procedural Nature: RA specifies how to retrieve the data by describing a sequence of operations. 
■Mathematical Core: Based on set theory, ensuring that every operation has a 
well-defined, predictable result. 
■Relational Algebra vs. SQL: RA provides the formal foundation that SQL engines use to build and optimize execution plans.

175/1260 
The Relational Model Relational Algebra (RA): The Procedural Language 
Fundamental Unary Operations: Selection (σ) 
■ Operator: O predicate (R) 
■ Purpose: Filters tuples (rows) based on a logical condition. It performs Horizontal Subsetting. 
■ Predicate Logic: Uses comparison operators (=, <, >,, <, >) and logical 
connectives (A, V, ¬). 
■ Properties: 
■The arity (columns) of the result is the same as the input. 
■The cardinality (rows) of the result is the input. 
■Commutativity: σA(OB (R)) = σB(σA(R)).

The Relational Model Relational Algebra (RA): The Procedural Language 
Fundamental Unary Operations: Projection (π) 
■ Operator: Tattr1, attr2....(R) 
■ Purpose: Selects specific attributes (columns) and discards the rest. It performs Vertical Subsetting. 
■Set-Theoretic Rigor: Because a relation is a set, π must eliminate duplicate tuples that may result from removing identifying attributes. 
■ Properties: 
■The arity of the result is equal to the number of attributes in the projection list. 
■The cardinality may be less than the input if duplicates are removed.

The Relational Model Relational Algebra (RA): The Procedural Language 
Fundamental Unary Operations: Renaming (p) 
■ Operator: Ps(B1,B2,...,Bn) (R) 
■ Purpose: Provides a new name for a relation and/or its attributes. It does not change the data, only the metadata (the signature). 
■Notation Variants: 
■ Ps(R): Changes the relation name to S, keeps attribute names. 
■ P(B1,B2,...)(R): Keeps relation name, changes attribute names. 
☐ Use Cases: 
1. Disambiguation: Resolving name conflicts when joining two tables that share 
identical column names but different meanings. 
2. Set Operations: Forcing compatibility by ensuring two relations have the exact 
same attribute names.

181/1260 
The Closure Property 
The Relational Model Relational Algebra (RA): The Procedural Language 
Definition 
The result of any relational algebra operation is itself a relation. 
■ This property allows for Nested Expressions (Composition). 
■ Example: πName(@Salary>50000 (Employee)) 
■ Significance: Closure is what makes the relational model scalable and allows for the creation of complex queries from a small set of primitive operators.

182/1260 
Binary Set Operations 
The Relational Model Relational Algebra (RA): The Procedural Language 
■ Union (U): Tuples in R, or S, or both. 
■ Intersection (n): Tuples present in both R and S. 
■ Set Difference (-): Tuples in R that are not in S. 
■ Type Compatibility (Union Compatibility): 
■R and S must have the same set of attributes. 
■ Intersection is usually derived from the other two: ROSER — (R − S).
