422/1260 
The Ontology of NULL 
Querying Theory Three-Valued Logic (3VL) & The NULL Problem 
■ Definition: NULL is not a value; it is a marker indicating the absence of a value. 
■ Semantics: SQL uses a single marker to represent multiple semantic concepts: 1. Unknown: The value exists in reality, but we do not know it (e.g., A user's 
birthdate is missing). 
2. Inapplicable: The attribute does not apply to this entity (e.g., "Spouse Name” for 
a single person). 
3. Withheld: The value is known but protected (Security). 
■ Consequence: Because NULL represents "Unknown," any arithmetic or logical operation involving NULL usually yields "Unknown.” 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2

■ Classical Boolean Logic has two states: True (T) and False (F). 
■ SQL Logic has three states: True, False, and Unknown (U).

424/1260 
Querying Theory Three-Valued Logic (3VL) & The NULL Problem 
The "True-at-the-End" Rule 
■ Mechanism: The WHERE and HAVING clauses act as filters. 
■The Rule: A row is included in the result set if and only if the predicate evaluates explicitly to True. 
■ Rejection: 
-> 
■False → Rejected. 
■ Unknown → Rejected. 
■ Implication: WHERE Age > 20 will exclude people under 20 and people with NULL 
ages. 
■ WHERE NOT (Age > 20) will also exclude people with NULL ages. The logical complement does not cover the full set.

425/1260 
Querying Theory Three-Valued Logic (3VL) & The NULL Problem 
Comparison Logic: The Equality Trap 
The Comparison Axiom 
If x is Unknown, then x = y is Unknown for any y (even if y is also Unknown). 
■ Code: SELECT * FROM T WHERE Col = NULL 
■Result: Empty Set. Always. 
■ Reason: NULL = NULL evaluates to Unknown, which is rejected by the filter. 
■ Correct Syntax: 
■ Check existence: WHERE Col IS NULL 
■ Check value: WHERE Col IS NOT NULL

426/1260 
Querying Theory Three-Valued Logic (3VL) & The NULL Problem 
Standard Null-Safe Equality 
■ Problem: How to compare two columns where both might be NULL, treating "NULL matches NULL" as True? 
■ Naive Method: 
■Standard Method: 
(A = B) OR (A IS NULL AND B IS NULL) 
A IS [NOT] DISTINCT FROM B 
■ Defaults to inequality; use the negative form (NOT DISTINCT FROM) for equality. 
■Treats NULL as a normal value for comparison purposes. 
■ Evaluates to strictly True or False (Two-Valued Logic).

Impact on Aggregation 
Querying Theory Three-Valued Logic (3VL) & The NULL Problem 
NULL handling changes based on the context of the aggregate function. 
Function 
COUNT(*) 
COUNT(expr) 
SUM(expr) 
AVG(expr) 
Behavior with NULLS 
Counts rows. NULLs in columns are ignored; the row exists, so it is counted. 
Counts values. Rows where expr is NULL are skipped. 
Ignores NULLs. If all values are NULL, returns NULL (not 0). 
Ignores NULLs in both numerator and denominator. 
■ Warning: AVG(expr) # SUM(expr) / COUNT(*). It is SUM(expr) / COUNT(expr).

428/1260 
The "Not Equal" Trap 
Querying Theory Three-Valued Logic (3VL) & The NULL Problem 
■ Scenario: We want to find users who are not in the Banned status. 
Data: 
■ Alice: Active 
■ Bob: Banned 
■Charlie: NULL (status not set) 
■Query: SELECT * FROM Users WHERE Status <> 'Banned' 
■ Analysis: 
■ Alice: 'Active' <> 'Banned' → True. (Returned) 
■ Bob: 'Banned' <> 'Banned' → False. (Skipped) 
■ Charlie: NULL <> 'Banned' → Unknown. (Skipped) 
■Result: Charlie is lost, even though he is technically "Not Banned."

430/1260 
Aggregates and NULL 
Querying Theory Three-Valued Logic (3VL) & The NULL Problem 
■Data: Price column = {10, 20, NULL, 30}. 
■ Query: SELECT AVG(Price) FROM Items 
■ Path A (Mathematically rigorous): Sum/Count(Rows). 
■ (10 + 20 + 30)/4 = 15. 
■ Path B (SQL Standard): Sum/Count(Values). 
■ (10+20 + 30)/3 = 20. 
■ Implication: AVG implicitly assumes NULL means "Does not exist" rather than "Zero." If NULL means "Free", you must use AVG(COALESCE(Price, 0)).

431/1260 
The CONCAT Anomaly 
Querying Theory Three-Valued Logic (3VL) & The NULL Problem 
■ Scenario: Constructing a full name. 
■ Query: SELECT First || '' || Middle || '' || Last 
■Data: First = 'John', Middle = NULL, Last = 'Doe'. 
■Standard SQL Result: NULL ("String || Unknown = Unknown.”). 
■ Vendor Variation: 
Oracle/PostgreSQL: result is NULL. 
■ SQL Server (CONCAT function): result is 'John Doe'. 
Lesson: String concatenation behavior with NULLS is inconsistent across engines; always use COALESCE or vendor-specific null-safe functions.

2025/26/2 
432/1260 
Querying Theory Aggregate Theory & Grouping 
Generalized Projection 
■ Standard Projection (π): Selects specific attributes (A1, A2) and eliminates duplicates. 
■ Generalized Projection (*): Allows the list of components to include functions or expressions over the attributes. 
■ Notation: πF1(A),F2(B)(R) 
■ Impact: This is the theoretical basis for SQL's ability to return calculated values. 
■ Arithmetic: SELECT Price* 1.2 
■ Aggregation: SELECT SUM(Price) 
■ Scalar vs. Aggregate: Scalar functions preserve cardinality (1 row→ 1 row). Aggregate functions reduce cardinality (N rows → 1 row). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
433/1260 
The Grouping Mechanism 
Querying Theory Aggregate Theory & Grouping 
■ SQL Clause: GROUP BY G1, G2, ... 
■ Operation: Partition the relation R into disjoint subsets (equivalence classes) S1, S2, ..., Sk such that all tuples in a subset S¿ agree on the values of the grouping attributes. 
■The Functional Dependency Rule: If a query contains a GROUP BY clause, every item in the SELECT list must be: 
1. Functionally dependent on expressions in the GROUP BY list, OR 
2. An aggregate function (e.g., SUM, COUNT) applied to the group. 
The DBMS Limitation 
The DBMS deduces some FDs but not others. Use the ANY_VALUE aggregate to "override" (technically, it is an aggregate per case #2). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
434/1260 
Querying Theory Aggregate Theory & Grouping 
Grouping Violation Example 
SELECT DeptID, EmpName, COUNT(*) 
FROM Employees 
GROUP BY DeptID 
■Logic Error: The group is defined by DeptID. Inside that group, there are multiple EmpName values (Alice, Bob). Which name should be returned? The engine cannot decide arbitrarily. 
Exception: Some engines (MySQL pre-5.7, SQLite) allow this by returning an arbitrary row, but this violates the SQL Standard and relational determinism. 
■ Valid Exception: If DeptID is a Primary Key, then DeptID → EmpName (Functional Dependency). Modern standards allow Name in SELECT because it is functionally dependent on the grouping key.

Conditional Logic: The CASE Expression 
Purpose: Implementing branching logic within a declarative statement (Data-dependent projection). 
■ Syntax: 
CASE WHEN \textit Predicate) THEN textit Value} ELSE textit{Value} END 
■Granularity: CASE operates at the granularity of the row context it is in. 
■ Evaluation: Most engines implement CASE as a strictly evaluated branch (short-circuiting). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
437/1260 
Querying Theory Aggregate Theory & Grouping 
Conditional Aggregation (The Pivot Pattern) 
■ Problem: Counting subsets of data (e.g., "Red Items" vs. "Blue Items") in a single pass without using subqueries. 
■ Solution: Nesting CASE inside an Aggregate. 
Example (Pivot Query) 
SELECT 
SUM(CASE WHEN Color = 'Red' THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) AS RedCount, 
SUM(CASE WHEN Color = 'Blue' THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) AS BlueCount FROM Items 
The same effect is achievable with the FILTER clause, but that is not supported by every DBMS. This is more universal.

438/1260 
The Semantics of DISTINCT 
Querying Theory Aggregate Theory & Grouping 
■Global DISTINCT: 
■ SELECT DISTINCT A, B FROM R 
■ Projects A, B and removes duplicate tuples. 
■Requires a Sort or Hash operation on the result set. 
■ Aggregate DISTINCT: 
■ COUNT(DISTINCT UserID) 
■ Counts unique values within the group. 
■ Cost: Requires maintaining a separate hash table or sort structure for each group, making it significantly more expensive than simple 'COUNT'.

447/1260 
Handling Nulls: COALESCE 
Querying Theory Conditional & Scalar Logic 
■ Definition: COALESCE(v1, v2, ..., vn) returns the first non-NULL value. 
■Standard: Part of ANSI SQL. 
■Type Safety: All arguments should be type-compatible (or implicit casting occurs). 
■Syntactic Sugar: It is shorthand for: 
CASE WHEN v1 IS NOT NULL THEN v1 ELSE v2 ... END 
Contrast: ISNULL(v1, v2) (SQL Server/Sybase) is non-standard and often has different type precedence rules. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
448/1260 
Handling Errors: NULLIF 
Querying Theory Conditional & Scalar Logic 
■ Definition: NULLIF(A, B) returns NULL if A = B, otherwise returns A. 
☐Primary Use Case: Preventing "Divide by Zero" errors. 
Example (Safe Division) 
SELECT Total / NULLIF(Count, 0) FROM Stats; 
■Logic: If Count is 0, NULLIF returns NULL. 
■Total / NULL results in NULL (Valid SQL), whereas Total / 0 throws a runtime exception. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
449/1260 
Querying Theory Conditional & Scalar Logic 
Horizontal Aggregation: GREATEST / LEAST 
■ Vertical: MAX(Expr) finds the maximum value of an expression across many rows. 
■ Horizontal: GREATEST(Expr1, Expr2, Expr3) finds the maximum value across columns within a single row. 
■NULL Handling: 
■MAX: Ignores NULLs. 
■GREATEST: Usually returns NULL if any argument is NULL (standard behavior, 
though implementation varies). 
■Use Case: "What is the most recent activity?" (GREATEST(Login Date, UpdateDate, CreatedDate)). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
450/1260 
Querying Theory Conditional & Scalar Logic 
Deterministic vs. Non-Deterministic Functions 
■ Deterministic: Always returns the same result for the same input. (e.g., UPPER(), ABS()). 
■ Non-Deterministic: May return different results for the same input. (e.g., NOW(), 
RAND()). 
■Theoretical Consequence: 
■ Dependency: The output of a deterministic function is functionally dependent on its input. 
■Indexing: You cannot create an index on a non-deterministic computed column (the index would "rot" instantly). 
■Caching: Query cache cannot store results involving CURRENT_TIMESTAMP. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
451/1260 
Querying Theory Conditional & Scalar Logic 
Short-Circuit Logic in Predicates 
■ Question: In WHERE A() AND B(), is B() executed if A() is false? 
■The SQL Standard: NO guarantee of execution order. The optimizer is free to reorder predicates for performance. 
■Risk: 
WHERE (Denom <> 0) AND (Num / Denom > 1) 
■ Optimization Hazard: If the optimizer decides Num / Denom is more selective, it may run it first, causing a Divide-by-Zero error. 
■Safe Guard: Use CASE for order-dependent logic, as CASE enforces evaluation order. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
452/1260 
Querying Theory Advanced Set Logic 
Set Operator Precedence 
■The Trap: Like arithmetic (× before +), Set Operators have a standard precedence order. 
■The Standard Rule: INTERSECT binds tighter (higher precedence) than UNION and EXCEPT. 
Example (Query Analysis) 
SELECT * FROM A 
UNION 
SELECT * FROM B 
INTERSECT 
SELECT * FROM C 
■ Interpretation: A U (B n C). 
Not: (AUB) n C. 
Best Practice: Always use parentheses to make intent explicit.

■ How does SQL calculate duplicate counts in Bag Operations?

Duplicate Count Result 
m + n 
1 if m + n > 0, else 0 
min(m, n)

max(0,m - n)

454/1260 
Querying Theory Advanced Set Logic 
The CORRESPONDING Clause 
■Standard SQL Behavior: Set operations match columns by Position (order in the SELECT list). 
■Risk: If Table A is (ID, Name) and Table B is (Name, ID), UNION will try to merge Names with IDs. 
■Solution (SQL Standard): 
SELECT * FROM A UNION CORRESPONDING SELECT * FROM B 
■Logic: Matches columns by Name, ignoring position. Columns found in only one table are discarded. 
Note: Support varies by vendor (PostgreSQL does not support this; requires explicit projection).

2025/26/2 
455/1260 
Querying Theory 
Advanced Set Logic 
Comparative Difference Logic 
■ Three ways to compute "Items in A but not B”. 
Method 
EXCEPT 
NOT IN 
LEFT JOIN ... NULL 
Nuance 
Set semantics. Removes duplicates. Handles NULL as a matching value (NULL matches NULL). 
Fails if B contains any NULL (returns empty set). 
Bag semantics. Preserves duplicates from A. Handles NULL based on join condition.

2025/26/2 
456/1260 
Querying Theory Advanced Set Logic 
Self-Joins and Permutations 
■ Scenario: Find all pairs of teams playing against each other. 
■Query: SELECT A.Name, B.Name FROM Teams A, Teams B 
■Filter Logic: 
Filter Options 
WHERE A.ID <> B.ID: Returns pairs (X, Y) and (Y, X). (Permutations). 
WHERE A.ID < B.ID: Returns only (X, Y). (Combinations). 
■Theory: A "Triangle Join" creates a strict ordering to eliminate reflexive and symmetric redundancy. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
457/1260 
The Conceptual View 
Querying Theory View Theory & Virtual Relations 
■ Definition: A View is a Virtual Relation defined by a query expression. 
Nature: 
■It has a name and a schema, appearing to the user like a standard table. 
■ Unlike a Base Table, it (usually) does not store physical tuples. 
■It stores the Logic (the query text) in the System Catalog. 
■ Execution: When a user queries a view, the DBMS performs Query Modification, substituting the view name with its definition in the parse tree (Macro expansion). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
458/1260 
Benefits of Views 
Querying Theory View Theory & Virtual Relations 
■Logical Data Independence: 
If the schema of a base table changes (e.g., splitting 'Name' into 'First', 'Last'), a view can reconstruct the old schema (First || ' ' || Last), keeping legacy 
applications working. 
■Security (Row/Column Level): 
■ Give users access to a View that excludes sensitive columns (e.g., 'Salary') or filters sensitive rows (e.g., WHERE DeptID = 'Public'), without granting access to the base table. 
■ Abstraction: Hiding complex Joins and Aggregates behind a simple table name. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
459/1260 
The View Update Problem 
Querying Theory View Theory & Virtual Relations 
■The Query: Can we perform INSERT, UPDATE, or DELETE operations on a View? 
■The Challenge: A View is a derived artifact. A modification to the artifact must be translated back to a modification of the Base Tables. 
■ Ambiguity: For many views, there is no single, unique translation. 
Example (The Projection Ambiguity) 
CREATE VIEW V AS SELECT Name FROM Employee 
INSERT INTO V VALUES ('Alice') 
To insert into Employee, we need an ID and Salary. What values should the DBMS invent? NULL? Defaults? If ID is PK, the insert fails. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
460/1260 
Limits of Updatability 
Querying Theory View Theory & Virtual Relations 
■ A view is theoretically updatable only if there is a isomorphic mapping between the view tuples and the base tuples. 
■Generally non-updatable scenarios: 
1. Aggregates: You cannot UPDATE V SET AvgSal = 60000. The system cannot know 
how to distribute the raise among the individual employees. 
2. Distinct: DISTINCT removes the mapping to specific source rows. 
3. Group By: Summarized data cannot be reversed. 
4. Set Operations: UNION creates ambiguity about which source table to target. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
461/1260 
Querying Theory View Theory & Virtual Relations 
The View Update Problem: Joins 
■ Scenario: View V joins Dept and Emp. 
■ UPDATE V SET DeptName = 'Engineering' WHERE EmpID= 101 
Interpretation A: Move Employee 101 to the existing 'Engineering' department. (Change FK in Emp). 
Interpretation B: Rename the department Employee 101 works for to 'Engineering'. (Change value in Dept). 
■Result: Most SQL engines forbid updates on Joined Views unless the update targets exactly one base table and preserves the key. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
462/1260 
Materialized Views 
Querying Theory View Theory & Virtual Relations 
■ Concept: Instead of computing the view on-the-fly, compute the result once and store it physically on disk. 
■Use Case: Data Warehousing and OLAP. Complex aggregates (e.g., "Sales by Quarter") that take hours to compute but are read frequently. 
The Trade-off: 
■Read Performance: Instant (Pre-computed). 
■ Write Performance: Slower (Must update base table + view). 
■ Data Freshness: The materialized view can become "stale" if the base tables change. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
463/1260 
Querying Theory View Theory & Virtual Relations 
Materialized View Maintenance 
How do we keep the cached result consistent with the source? 
■ 1. Immediate Maintenance (Eager): 
■ Every write to the base table triggers an update to the view within the same transaction. 
Guarantees consistency but slows down OLTP throughput. 
■2. Deferred Maintenance (Lazy): 
■ Updates are batched or scheduled (e.g., "Refresh every night"). 
■Reads may see stale data. 
3. Incremental Maintenance: 
Computing only the delta (AV) based on the change (AR) rather than re-computing the full query. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
464/1260 
The Theoretical Barrier 
Querying Theory View Theory & Virtual Relations 
■ Why are most views read-only? 
■The Inversion Principle: For a view update to be valid, the DBMS must be able to find a unique set of updates to the base tables such that the modified view would produce the exact result requested by the user. 
Function: V = Q(Base). 
■ Requirement: We need Q-1(Vnew) to be deterministic. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
465/1260 
Querying Theory View Theory & Virtual Relations 
Case 1: The Constant Ratio Problem 
■ View: SELECT Dept, SUM(Salary) as Total FROM Emp GROUP BY Dept. 
■ Update: UPDATE V SET Total = 200000 WHERE Dept = 'Sales'. 
■Current State: Sales has 2 employees (Alice: 50k, Bob: 50k). Total = 100k. 
The Math: We need to increase the sum by 100k. 
1. Give Alice +100k? 
2. Give Bob +100k? 
3. Give both +50k? 
4. Fire Bob and hire Carol at 150k? 
Conclusion: Infinite solutions exist. The mapping is not bijective. Update rejected. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
466/1260 
Querying Theory View Theory & Virtual Relations 
Case 2: The Disappearing Row 
View: CREATE VIEW V AS SELECT * FROM Items WHERE Price < 10. 
■Check Option: SQL allows WITH CHECK OPTION. 
Scenario: UPDATE V SET Price = 20 WHERE ID = 5. 
☐ Without Check Option: 
■The update succeeds in the base table. 
■The row immediately vanishes from the view (because 2010). 
■This is a confusing UX ("I updated it, and now it's gone"). 
With Check Option: The update fails because the resulting row would not satisfy the view's WHERE clause.

2025/26/2 
467/1260 
Case 3: Union Views 
Querying Theory View Theory & Virtual Relations 
View: SELECT * FROM Archive UNION ALL SELECT * FROM Current. 
■Insert: INSERT INTO V VALUES (...). 
■ Problem: Which table gets the new row? 
■Solution (Partitioned Views): 
■If constraints exist (e.g., Archive has CHECK(Year < 2020) and Current has CHECK(Year >= 2020)), the DBMS can determine the target logic. 
■If constraints are missing, the update is ambiguous and rejected.

2025/26/2 
468/1260 
Querying Theory Data Modification Logic (DML) 
The Semantics of INSERT INTO ... SELECT 
■ Operation: Set-based insertion. 
■ Atomicity: The entire set of rows is inserted as a single transaction unit. If one row fails (e.g., PK violation), the entire batch rolls back. 
■Self-Reference: 
INSERT INTO T SELECT * FROM T 
Isolation: Standard SQL requires that the SELECT "sees" the state of T at the start of the statement. Infinite recursion (inserting rows that are then selected and inserted again) is prevented by Snapshot semantics. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
469/1260 
Querying Theory Data Modification Logic (DML) 
The MERGE Statement (Upsert) 
■ Goal: "Insert if new, Update if exists." 
■Standard Syntax: 
Example (MERGE INTO syntax) 
MERGE INTO Target T USING Source S ON (T.ID = S.ID) 
WHEN MATCHED THEN UPDATE SET ... 
WHEN NOT MATCHED THEN INSERT ... 
■Concurrency Limit: MERGE is often not atomic regarding concurrency. Two concurrent Merges can typically cause a Race Condition (Duplicate Key Error] unless explicit table locks are taken. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
470/1260 
Querying Theory Data Modification Logic (DML) 
Returning Data: RETURNING / OUTPUT 
■ Problem: After an INSERT, how do we know the auto-generated ID? 
■ Old Way: SELECT MAX(ID) (Unsafe/Race Condition). 
■Relational Way: Projection of the modified tuples. 
■ Syntax: 
INSERT INTO Users... RETURNING ID, CreatedAt 
■Logic: Treating the DML operation as a relation-producing operator.

471/1260 
Querying Theory Data Modification Logic (DML) 
Physical vs. Logical Deletion: TRUNCATE 
Command 
DELETE 
TRUNCATE 
Semantics 
Logical Row Deletion. Fires triggers. Logs every row. Respects FKs. Slow for massive tables. 
Physical Reset. Deallocates data pages. Minimal logging. Resets Identity counters. Cannot function if FKs point to the table. 
■Note: TRUNCATE is technically DDL in some engines (implicit commit), preventing rollback.

472/1260 
Querying Theory Data Modification Logic (DML) 
Advanced DML: Modifying via CTEs 
) 
■The Pattern: Using a CTE to pre-calculate a target set before performing a mutation (DELETE or UPDATE). 
■Logical Decomposition: Separates the "Search Strategy" (finding the 100 oldest logs) from the "Action" (deleting them). 
■Bypassing Dialect Limits: Bypasses errors like MySQL's Error 1093, which forbids selecting from a table you are currently modifying. 
■ Determinism: Forces the engine to "snapshot" the target IDs in memory, preventing the LIMIT logic from drifting during execution. 
Example (The Deletion Pattern) 
WITH target_set AS ( 
SELECT id FROM logs ORDER BY created_at ASC LIMIT 100 
DELETE FROM logs 
WHERE id IN (SELECT id FROM target_set);

473/1260 
Querying Theory Subqueries & Compositional Logic 
The Principle of Compositionality 
■Closure Property (Recalled): Since the input to a relational operator is a relation and 
the output is a relation, queries can be nested arbitrarily. 
■Subquery: A query nested inside another query. 
■Locations: 
■FROM clause: Acts as a derived table/view. 
■ WHERE/HAVING clause: Acts as a predicate or value provider. 
■ SELECT clause: Acts as a computed column. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
474/1260 
Querying Theory Subqueries & Compositional Logic 
Scalar vs. Table Subqueries 
Scalar Subquery 
■ Returns exactly one row and one column (a single atomic value). 
■Usage: Anywhere a constant is allowed (e.g., WHERE Price > (SELECT AVG(Price)...)). 
■Runtime Error: If it returns > 1 row, the query fails. 
Table Subquery 
■Returns zero or more rows with one or more columns. 
Usage: In FROM clauses or set-membership predicates (e.g., WHERE ID IN (SELECT ID...)). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
475/1260 
Correlation and Scope 
Querying Theory Subqueries & Compositional Logic 
■ Independent Subquery: 
■Does not reference the outer query. 
■Can be executed once, cached, and treated as a constant. 
■ Correlated Subquery: 
■References a column from the outer query (The "Correlation Name"). 
■ Execution Logic: Re-evaluated for every row of the outer query (conceptually a Nested Loop). 
■ Scope Rule: Inner queries can see Outer columns; Outer queries cannot see Inner columns (Lexical Scoping). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
476/1260 
Quantifiers: EXISTS (3) 
Querying Theory Subqueries & Compositional Logic 
■Syntax: WHERE EXISTS (Subquery) 
■ Semantics: Returns True if the subquery returns at least one row. 
■ Optimization: It is a "Semi-join." The engine stops scanning the subquery as soon as the first match is found. 
■Input Independence: The specific columns selected in the subquery do not matter. SELECT 1 is conventionally used, but SELECT * works identically. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
477/1260 
Querying Theory Subqueries & Compositional Logic 
The NOT IN Trap (Nulls Revisited) 
Theory Hazard 
NOT IN is not always equivalent to NOT EXISTS. 
■ Scenario: Subquery S returns {1, 2, NULL}. 
■Query: WHERE 5 NOT IN S. 
■ Expansion: 5 #1A52A5 NULL. 
■3VL Result: TATAU Unknown. 
■ Outcome: The row is discarded. If the subquery contains any NULLS, NOT IN returns an Empty Set for everyone. 
■Fix: Use NOT EXISTS, which handles NULLS correctly (Logic: "Does a matching row exist?" No row matches NULL via equality). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
478/1260 
Querying Theory Subqueries & Compositional Logic 
Set Comparisons: ANY and ALL 
■> ANY (S): True if the value is greater than the minimum value in S (standard 3). 
■ Equivalent to > (SELECT MIN(...) FROM S). 
> ALL (S): True if the value is greater than the maximum value in S (standard V). 
■ Equivalent to > (SELECT MAX(...) FROM S). 
■Note: = ANY is equivalent to IN.

2025/26/2 
479/1260 
Querying Theory Subqueries & Compositional Logic 
Common Table Expressions (CTEs) 
■Syntax: WITH Name AS (Query) SELECT... 
■ Purpose: Logical decomposition. Breaks large queries into readable, named blocks. 
■Scope: Defined locally for the statement. 
■ Materialization: 
■ Theoretically, a CTE is just a macro (inline expansion). 
■In practice (PostgreSQL, etc.), CTEs can act as optimization fences or be materialized (cached) if referenced multiple times.

2025/26/2 
480/1260 
Querying Theory Subqueries & Compositional Logic 
Recursive CTEs 
■Finally implementing the Datalog power in SQL. 
■ Anatomy: 
1. Anchor Member: The base case (e.g., 
SELECT * FROM Emp WHERE Manager IS NULL). 
2. UNION ALL 
3. Recursive Member: Joins the CTE name to the base table (e.g., 
JOIN Emp ON Emp.Mgr = CTE.ID). 
Execution: 
■R_0← Anchor. 
■Ri+1 R¡ Base. 
■Repeat until Ri+1 is empty. 
■This computes the transitive closure. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
481/1260 
Variable Scoping Rules 
Querying Theory Subqueries & Compositional Logic 
■SQL follows lexical scoping rules similar to programming languages. 
■ Inner Capture: An inner query can see columns from the outer query. 
■ Shadowing: If an inner query defines an alias identical to an outer column, the inner one takes precedence. 
The "Accidental Correlation" Disaster 
DELETE FROM Customers WHERE ID IN ( 
SELECT ID FROM BadList 
); 
■Scenario: BadList table has column B_ID, but no ID column. 
Result: The parser binds ID in the subquery to Customers.ID (outer reference). 
■Logic: WHERE C.ID IN (SELECT C.ID ...) is always true (if BadList is not empty). Outcome: Deletes every customer. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
482/1260 
Querying Theory Subqueries & Compositional Logic 
Scalar Subquery Performance 
■ Query: 
SELECT Name, (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM Orders O WHERE O.C_ID = C.ID) FROM Customers C. 
■Naive Execution: Execute the count query N times (once per customer). O(N · M). 
■ Optimization (Decorrelation): 
■The optimizer transforms this into a LEFT OUTER JOIN with a GROUP BY. 
■ Cost becomes O(N + M) (Hash Join). 
■Lesson: Correlated subqueries are declarative; do not assume they execute iteratively just because they look like loops.

483/1260 
Advanced Query Logic 
Topic 7 
Advanced Query Logic The Limit of Relational Algebra: Recursion & Fixed Points 
The Limits of Standard SQL 
■Recap: We established in Topic 3 and 4 that Relational Algebra (RA) is powerful but finite. 
■ The Reachability Problem: 
■ Given a table Parent(ParentID, ChildID), find all ancestors of 'Alice'. 
■RA Approach: 
■ Parents: πp (Oc='Alice' (R)) 
■Grandparents: Join R once. 
■ Great-Grandparents: Join R twice. 
■The Failure: To find ancestors of arbitrary depth N, we need a query of length N. 
Since N is unknown at query time, RA cannot express this (it is not Turing Complete). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
485/1259 
Transitive Closure 
Advanced Query Logic The Limit of Relational Algebra: Recursion & Fixed Points 
Definition 
The transitive closure of a binary relation R is the set of all pairs (x, y) such that there exists a sequence of elements Z1, Z2, ..., Zk where: 
E 
€ 
(x, Z1) E R, (Z1, Z2) Є R, ..., (Zk, y) Є R 
■ Graph Theory Equivalent: Finding all nodes reachable from a start node. 
■Solution: We need a mechanism to apply a rule repeatedly until no new facts are found. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
486/1259 
Advanced Query Logic The Limit of Relational Algebra: Recursion & Fixed Points 
Fixed-Point Theory (The Math) 
■ To solve recursion formally, we treat the query as a function T. 
■ The Operator (T): A function mapping a relation instance to another relation instance. 
■Fixed Point: A relation R is a fixed point of T if T(R) = R. 
■Least Fixed Point (LFP): The smallest relation R (subset-wise) that satisfies T(R) = R. 
■ Algorithm: 
■ R。 = 0 
■ Ri+1 = T(Ri) 
■Stop when Ri+1 = Ri. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
487/1259 
Monotonicity 
Advanced Query Logic 
The Limit of Relational Algebra: Recursion & Fixed Points 
■ For the LFP algorithm to guarantee termination on finite domains, the function T must be Monotonic. 
■Definition: If RCS, then T(R) ≤ T(S). 
■Intuition: Adding more rows to the input never causes rows to be removed from the output. 
■ Implication: We can only strictly grow the result set. If we allowed negation (-) or set difference inside the recursion, the set could shrink, potentially causing an infinite oscillation (no fixed point). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
488/1259 
Advanced Query Logic The Limit of Relational Algebra: Recursion & Fixed Points 
Recursive CTES: The SQL Implementation 
■ SQL implements Fixed-Point logic via WITH RECURSIVE. 
Example (Syntax Anatomy) 
WITH RECURSIVE Hierarchy AS ( 
) 
-- 1. Anchor Member (Base Case) 
SELECT ID, Name, O as Level FROM Emp WHERE Boss IS NULL UNION ALL 
-- 2. Recursive Member (Inductive Step) 
SELECT E.ID, E.Name, H.Level + 1 
= 
FROM Emp E JOIN Hierarchy H ON E.Boss H.ID 
SELECT * FROM Hierarchy; 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
489/1259 
Execution Semantics 
Advanced Query Logic The Limit of Relational Algebra: Recursion & Fixed Points 
1. Initialization: Execute the Anchor Member. Store result in a Working Table (W) and 
the Result Table (R). 
2. Recursion: While W is not empty: 
■ Execute the Recursive Member, replacing the reference to the CTE name with W. 
■The output becomes the new W. 
Append the new W to R. 
3. Termination: When the Recursive Member returns 0 rows (Working Table is empty), 
the loop breaks. 
■This naturally implements a Breadth-First Search (BFS). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
490/1259 
Advanced Query Logic The Limit of Relational Algebra: Recursion & Fixed Points 
Linear vs. Non-Linear Recursion 
■Linear recursion: The recursive definition references the CTE name exactly once. 
■ Ancestors (x, y) ← Parent(x, z), Ancestors (z, y). 
■"An ancestor of a parent is an ancestor." 
■ Easily computable. 
■ Supported by standard SQL engines. 
■Non-linear recursion: The recursive definition references the CTE multiple times. 
■ Friend(x, y) ← Friend(x, z), Friend(z, y). 
■"A friend of a friend is a friend." 
■ Much harder to compute (exponential explosion). Most SQL engines restrict users to linear recursion. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
491/1259 
Advanced Query Logic The Limit of Relational Algebra: Recursion & Fixed Points 
Cycle Detection (Path Arrays) 
■ Problem: If the graph has a cycle (A → B → A), the Fixed Point algorithm loops infinitely (or hits max recursion depth). 
■ Solution: Track the path history within the recursion. 
Example (Path Tracking Logic) 
SELECT ..., Path || ID as Path 
FROM ... 
WHERE ID <> ALL(Path) 
■ We carry the array of visited nodes and stop if we encounter a node already in our history. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
492/1259 
Stratified Negation 
Advanced Query Logic The Limit of Relational Algebra: Recursion & Fixed Points 
■The conflict: We learned about Datalog that recursion + negation = possible paradox. 
■ SQL constraint: You generally cannot put the following inside the recursive member: 
■NOT EXISTS / NOT IN 
■ ЕХСЕРТ 
■ aggregates (SUM, MAX) or DISTINCT 
■Reason: These operators are non-monotonic. They rely on the "complete" set of data to be accurate, but in recursion, the set is still growing. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
493/1259 
Advanced Query Logic The Limit of Relational Algebra: Recursion & Fixed Points 
Stratification: The Solution 
To handle negation, the query is divided into strata (layers). 
■ Stratum 1: Compute the recursive fixed point entirely (reachability). 
■Stratum 2: Apply negation/aggregation to the result of Stratum 1. 
■ Example: 
■invalid: "Find people who are NOT ancestors of X" (inside the loop). 
■valid: "Calculate all ancestors of X" (CTE). 
Then SELECT * FROM People WHERE ID NOT IN (SELECT ID FROM CTE).

503/1259 
Advanced Query Logic 
Order-Aware Algebra: Window Functions 
Sets vs. Sequences 
■The Orthodoxy: The Relational Model (Topic 3) treats relations as sets. Sets have no intrinsic order. {A, B} is identical to {B,A}. 
■The Problem: Real-world questions often rely on sequence. 
■"What is the 3-day moving average of stock prices?" 
■"Who are the top 3 runners per country?" 
■ "What was the value of this sensor previous to the current reading?" 
■The Old Solution: Self-Joins with complex inequalities (T1.date > T2.date). This is O(N2). 
■The New Solution: Window Functions (SQL:2003). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
504/1259 
The Window Mechanism 
Advanced Query Logic Order-Aware Algebra: Window Functions 
■ Core Concept: Perform calculations across a set of table rows that are somehow related to the current row. 
■ Difference from Aggregates: 
■ GROUP BY: Collapses the group into a single row (Cardinality Reduction). 
■ WINDOW: Preserves the original rows, adding a derived column (Cardinality Preservation). 
■Syntax: 
Function() OVER (PARTITION BY ... ORDER BY ... Frame) 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
505/1259 
Advanced Query Logic Order-Aware Algebra: Window Functions 
Partitioning and Ordering 
■ PARTITION BY: Divides the result set into buckets. The window function resets for each partition. 
■ Analogy: Like GROUP BY, but without hiding the details. 
■ ORDER BY: Defines the logical sequence of rows within the partition. 
■ Essential for ranking and running totals. 
■ Without this, the concept of "Previous Row" is undefined. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
506/1259 
Advanced Query Logic Order-Aware Algebra: Window Functions 
The Frame Clause: Defining Visibility 
■The Frame defines the specific subset of the partition visible to the current row for 
calculation. 
■Syntax: 
■ Boundaries: 
[ROWS | RANGE] BETWEEN Start AND End 
■ UNBOUNDED PRECEDING: Start of partition. 
■N PRECEDING: N units back. 
■ CURRENT ROW: The row itself. 
■N FOLLOWING: N units forward. 
■ UNBOUNDED FOLLOWING: End of partition.

Database Systems 
Use Case 
Moving averages (fixed window size regardless of value). 
Time-series (e.g., ”last 7 days” even if some days are missing). 
Logical ranking analysis. 
2025/26/2 
508/1259 
Advanced Query Logic 
Order-Aware Algebra: Window Functions 
The "Peer" Problem 
Implicit Default Warning 
If ORDER BY is specified but no Frame is defined, the default is: RANGE BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW 
■ Peers: Rows with identical values in the ORDER BY columns. 
■ Behavior: 
■ROWS: Processes peers one by one (arbitrary tie-break). Cumulative sum increases per row (10, 20, 30). 
■RANGE: Processes peers simultaneously. Cumulative sum jumps (30, 30, 30). 
■ Performance: RANGE is often slower because the engine must look ahead to detect ties. Use ROWS if uniqueness is guaranteed. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2

■ Values: 10, 10, 20, 30

510/1259 
Algebraic Extension 
Advanced Query Logic Order-Aware Algebra: Window Functions 
■ Dependent Projection: Mathematically, Window Functions extend the Projection operator (π). 
■ Standard π: ƒ(t) depends only on attributes within tuple t. 
■ Window π: f(t) depends on a set of tuples Wt, where Wt is defined by the Partition and Frame logic relative to t. 
■ Optimization: 
■Requires a Sort operator on the Partition keys + Order keys. 
■Can be pipelined: As soon as a row and its frame are loaded, the result is emitted.

516/1259 
Advanced Query Logic The Windowing Lens: A Moving Frame Trace 
Logic Check: ROWS vs RANGE 
■What if Day 3 was missing? (Gaps in data). 
■ROWS: The engine physically counts back 2 rows. 
■Frame for Day 4 would include Day 1 and Day 2. 
■RANGE: The engine looks for values Day - 2. 
■Frame for Day 4 would look for Day 2 and Day 3. 
■ Since Day 3 is missing, it averages only [Day2, Day4].

517/1259 
Advanced Query Logic Pattern Matching 
Row Pattern Recognition: MATCH_RECOGNIZE 
■Goal: Identify sequential patterns (shapes, trends, events) across an ordered stream of rows. 
■The SQL:2016 Standard: Introduced MATCH_RECOGNIZE to solve complex 
temporal/sequential queries without deep self-joins or convoluted Window Functions. 
■Use Cases: 
■Finance: Finding "V-shape" recoveries or "Double Bottom" patterns. 
■ Security: Detecting brute-force login attempts followed by a success. 
■loT/Telemetry: Identifying sensor spikes or degradation over time. 
■ Mechanism: Applies Finite State Machines (Regular Expressions) to ordered partitions of rows. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
518/1259 
The Clause Pipeline 
Advanced Query Logic Pattern Matching 
■MATCH_RECOGNIZE operates as a highly structured pipeline applied to a table. 
1. PARTITION BY & ORDER BY: Groups and sorts the input stream. 
2. MEASURES: Defines the output columns (calculated from the matched rows). 
3. [ONE | ALL] ROWS PER MATCH: Determines output cardinality. 
4. AFTER MATCH SKIP TO...: Tells the engine where to resume searching after a match is 
found. 
5. PATTERN: The regular expression defining the sequence of row variables. 
6. DEFINE: The boolean conditions that map real rows to pattern variables. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
519/1259 
Advanced Query Logic Pattern Matching 
Syntax Anatomy: Finding a V-Shape 
Example (V-Shape Price Recovery) 
SELECT * FROM Ticker MATCH_RECOGNIZE ( 
PARTITION BY Symbol ORDER BY Time 
MEASURES 
MATCH_NUMBER() AS MatchID, START_ROW. Price AS StartPrice, LAST(DOWN.Price) AS Bottom Price, LAST(UP.Price) AS EndPrice 
ONE ROW PER MATCH 
AFTER MATCH SKIP TO LAST UP 
PATTERN (START_ROW DOWN+ UP+) 
DEFINE 
DOWN AS DOWN.Price< PREV(DOWN.Price), UP AS UP.Price > PREV(UP.Price) 
) AS MR; 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
520/1259 
Advanced Query Logic Pattern Matching 
The PATTERN Clause: Regex for Rows 
■The PATTERN defines the exact sequence of Row Pattern Variables. 
■Regex Quantifiers Supported: 
■* : 0 or more times. 
■+ : 1 or more times. 
?0 or 1 time. 
{n, m}: Between n and m times. 
■Greedy vs. Lazy: 
■By default, * and + are Greedy (they consume as many valid rows as possible). 
■ Appending? makes them Lazy (e.g., *? stops at the first valid subsequent match to keep the overall pattern as short as possible). 
■ Anchors: ^ (Start of partition) and $ (End of partition). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
521/1259 
Advanced Query Logic Pattern Matching 
The DEFINE Clause: Classifying Rows 
■Maps incoming rows to the variables used in PATTERN using boolean expressions. 
■ Variables without a definition (like START_ROW in our example) are assumed to be always true (matches any row). 
■ Navigation Functions: Allow checking adjacent rows during evaluation. 
■ PREV(X.Price): The price of the row immediately preceding the current row. 
■ NEXT(X.Price): The price of the row immediately following. 
■ Example: DOWN AS DOWN.Price< PREV(DOWN.Price) 
■"A row is classified as DOWN if its price is strictly less than the row immediately before it." 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
522/1259 
Advanced Query Logic Pattern Matching 
Extracting Data: MEASURES 
■ Computes values based on the group of rows that successfully formed the match. 
■ Pattern-Specific Aggregates: 
■FIRST(X.Col): The value from the first row mapped to variable X in the current match. 
■LAST(X.Col): The value from the last row mapped to variable X. 
■Built-in Metadata Functions: 
■MATCH_NUMBER(): A sequentially generated ID for each distinct match found in the partition. 
■ CLASSIFIER(): Returns the string name of the variable that the current row matched (highly useful when using ALL ROWS PER MATCH). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
523/1259 
Advanced Query Logic Pattern Matching 
Overlapping Matches: AFTER MATCH SKIP 
■ When a match is found, where should the engine look for the next pattern? 
■ Options: 
■ SKIP PAST LAST ROW: (Default) Resumes evaluating strictly after the current match ends. Matches will never share rows (disjoint). 
■SKIP TO NEXT ROW: Resumes searching starting one row after the start of the current match. Allows heavily overlapping matches. 
■SKIP TO LAST X: Resumes execution at the last row matched by the specified variable X. 
■ Why it matters: In a "W-shape” market trend, the upward leg of the first 'V' is also the starting point of the second 'V'. Using SKIP TO LAST UP ensures the system captures both. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
524/1259 
Advanced Query Logic Pattern Matching 
Execution Logic & Performance 
1. Sort: The engine sorts the partition by the ORDER BY columns. 
2. Evaluate: It feeds rows sequentially into a non-deterministic finite automaton (NFA) 
built from the PATTERN. 
3. Buffer & Backtrack: If a greedy operator consumes too many rows and prevents the 
next part of the pattern from matching, the engine buffers rows and backtracks to try alternative match lengths. 
4. Emit: Once the pattern completes, it calculates the MEASURES and emits the result 
based on the row cardinality setting. 
■ Performance: This state-machine logic is significantly faster than traditional Self-Joins or nested LAG()/LEAD() functions because it evaluates complex, multi-row sequences in a single, localized pass over the data.

2025/26/2

Advanced Query Logic Universal Quantification: Implementing Relational Division 
The "For All" (V) Challenge 
■The Logical Goal: To answer queries involving the Universal Quantifier. 
■Common phrasing: "Find X that are associated with all Y in set S." 
■ Examples: 
■"Find students who have taken every Biology course." 
"Find pilots who are certified to fly all Boeing planes." 
■ "Find projects that use all available part types." 
■The SQL Gap: Standard SQL has no direct 'DIVIDE BY' or 'FOR ALL' operator. We must simulate it.

Strategy A: Cardinality Comparison 
■The Logic: If the number of distinct target items a student has matches the total number of target items, they must have them all. 
■ Formula: 
■ Prerequisites: 
|Student's Matches| = |Total Requirements| 
1. Uniqueness (Set semantics) must be enforced. 2. The "Total" count must be computable.

Advanced Query Logic Universal Quantification: Implementing Relational Division 
Strategy A: SQL Implementation 
Example (The "Count Hack") 
SELECT StudentID 
FROM Taken T JOIN Required R ON T.CourseID = R.CourseID 
GROUP BY StudentID 
HAVING COUNT(DISTINCT T.CourseID) = ( 
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM Required 
); 
■ Pros: Easy to read and write. Efficient for small target sets. 
■ Cons: 'COUNT(DISTINCT)' can be expensive. Fails if the relation involves duplicates or complex conditions not mappable to simple equality.

The "Empty Requirement" Edge Case 
■ Scenario: What if the Required table is empty? 
■Logic: 
■ The set of "Missing Courses" is Ø for everyone (even students with no courses). 
■ NOT EXISTS(Ø) is always True. 
■ Outcome: Every student is returned. 
■ Mathematical Truth: "Alice has taken every course in the empty set" is a Vacuously True statement.

537/1259 
Advanced Query Logic Lateral Logic & Dependent Joins 
The Scope Problem in FROM 
■Standard SQL Rule: Tables in the FROM clause are evaluated independently before being joined. 
■ Implication: A subquery in the FROM clause cannot reference attributes from a preceding table in the same list. 
Invalid Query Example 
SELECT C.Name, O.RecentDate 
FROM Customers C, 
(SELECT MAX(Date) FROM Orders WHERE UserID = C.ID) AS O 
■ Error: C.ID is not visible inside the subquery scope because C and the subquery are conceptual peers, not nested scopes. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
538/1259 
Advanced Query Logic 
Lateral Logic & Dependent Joins 
The LATERAL Keyword 
■ Definition: LATERAL (standard SQL, PostgreSQL) or CROSS APPLY (SQL Server, Oracle) creates a Dependent Join. 
■Logic: It allows the right-hand side subquery to reference columns from the 
left-hand side item. 
■ Execution Paradigm: 
■ For each row produced by the left table... 
■ ... execute the right subquery (using the left row's values as parameters) ... 
■ ... join the results. 
■This essentially forces a Nested Loop iteration logic at the semantic level. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
539/1259 
Advanced Query Logic Lateral Logic & Dependent Joins 
Pattern 1: Top-N-Per-Group 
■Goal: Find the top 3 most recent orders for every customer. 
■ Without Lateral: Requires Window Functions (ROW_NUMBER()) or complex correlated subqueries in WHERE. 
■ With Lateral: We write a query to find the top 3 orders for one customer, then apply it to all. 
Example (The Query) 
SELECT C.Name, 0.* 
FROM Customers C CROSS JOIN LATERAL ( 
SELECT * FROM Orders WHERE UserID = C.ID ORDER BY Date DESC LIMIT 3 
)0

Pattern 2: Set-Returning Functions (Unnesting) 
■ Scenario: A column contains a complex type (Array or JSON) that needs to be normalized into rows.

SELECT D.ID, T.Tag

■The UNNEST function runs dependent on the current D.Tags value.

542/1259 
Lateral Outer Joins 
Advanced Query Logic 
Lateral Logic & Dependent Joins 
■Syntax: LEFT JOIN LATERAL ... ON TRUE 
■ Purpose: To preserve the left-hand row even if the subquery returns empty. 
■Top-N Example: 
■CROSS JOIN LATERAL: Removes Customers who have zero orders. 
■LEFT JOIN LATERAL: Keeps Customers with zero orders (Columns from 'O' will be NULL). 
■This enables "optional" complex sub-calculations per row.

543/1259 
Advanced Query Logic Multidimensional Logic: The Data Cube 
Beyond 2D: The Multidimensional Shift 
■The Relational Limit: Standard SQL GROUP BY aggregates data into a single, flat set of disjoint groups (a 2D projection). 
■The OLAP Requirement: Decision support systems require viewing data from multiple angles simultaneously (e.g., "Sales by Year," "Sales by Region," AND "Sales by Year and Region"). 
■The Data Cube: A logical model where data is viewed as an N-dimensional hypercube. 
■ Super-Aggregation: The database must compute Subtotals and Grand Totals alongside the granular data in a single result set. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
544/1259 
The Aggregation Lattice 
Advanced Query Logic Multidimensional Logic: The Data Cube 
■Concept: For a set of grouping dimensions D = {A, B, C), the Aggregation Lattice is 
the power set of D. 
■ Example: Dimensions Region, Product. 
■The Lattice Nodes: 
1. (Region, Product): Base granularity. 
2. (Region): Subtotals per region. 
3. (Product): Subtotals per product. 
4. (): The Grand Total (Empty set). 
■Goal: To compute all or some of these nodes efficiently in one pass. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
545/1259 
Advanced Query Logic Multidimensional Logic: The Data Cube 
Hierarchical Aggregation: ROLLUP 
■Logic: Generates grouping sets based on a strictly hierarchical ordering. 
■Syntax: GROUP BY ROLLUP (A, B, C) 
■Result: Generates N + 1 grouping sets: 
1. (A, B, C) 
2. (A, B) 
3. (A) 4.0 
■Use Case: Time or Geography hierarchies (e.g., Country → State → City). It makes no sense to group by City without the State context.

Advanced Query Logic Multidimensional Logic: The Data Cube 
Selective Aggregation: GROUPING SETS 
■Logic: Allows specific, manual definition of the desired groups. 
■ Syntax: GROUP BY GROUPING SETS ((A, B), (A), ()) 
■ Algebraic Equivalent: 
SELECT A, B, SUM(X)... GROUP BY A, B 
UNION ALL 
SELECT A, NULL, SUM(X)... GROUP BY A 
UNION ALL 
SELECT NULL, NULL, SUM(X) ... 
■ Benefit: The engine optimizes this into a single scan of the data, whereas the UNION 
approach requires N scans.

551/1259 
Advanced Query Logic 
Multidimensional Logic: The Data Cube 
The NULL Ambiguity 
The Problem 
In a CUBE result, a NULL value in a grouping column can mean two things: 
1. The "All" Marker: This row is a subtotal (e.g., Total for All Regions). 
2. The Data Value: The source data actually contained a NULL (e.g., a sale with no Region 
specified). 
■ Solution: The GROUPING(Col) function. 
■ Return Value: 
■ 1: The NULL is a super-aggregate placeholder. 
■0: The value is from the data (even if it is NULL). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
552/1259 
Advanced Query Logic Multidimensional Logic: The Data Cube 
Using GROUPING() for Display 
Best Practice: When generated for human consumption, always label the NULLs clearly. 
Example (SQL Pattern) 
SELECT 
CASE WHEN GROUPING(Region) = 1 
THEN 'ALL REGIONS' 
ELSE Region END AS RegionName, 
SUM(Sales) 
FROM Sales Table 
GROUP BY ROLLUP(Region) 
This converts the abstract NULL marker into a human-readable label in the application layer.

Advanced Query Logic Formatting Logic: Pivot and Unpivot 
Relational vs. Reporting Layouts 
■ Relational (Long/Narrow): (Year, Quarter, Sales). Good for storage and filtering. 
■Reporting (Wide/Cross-tab): Years as Rows, Quarters as Columns. Good for human reading. 
■PIVOT: Rotates unique values in a column into multiple columns. 
■ UNPIVOT: Rotates column headers into values of a single column (Normalization).

560/1259 
Advanced Query Logic Temporal Query Logic 
Time as a Logical Primitive 
■The Snapshot Assumption: Standard Relational Databases model the "Current State" of reality. Old values are overwritten (destructively updated). 
■The Temporal Reality: "Truth" is a function of time. Fact(x) may be true at t1 but false at t2. 
■Interval Representation: 
■Time is rarely a point; it is a duration. 
■ Closed-Open Convention [S, E): Starts at S, continues up to (but not including) E. 
■ Benefit: E1 = S2 implies adjacency (no gap, no overlap). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
561/1259 
Advanced Query Logic 
Temporal Query Logic 
Bi-temporal Modeling 
To accurately model history, we often track two independent time dimensions: 
1. Valid Time (Application Time) 
The time period during which a fact was true in the real world. 
■ Example: "Alice's promotion is effective from Jan 1 to Dec 31." 
2. Transaction Time (System Time) 
The time period during which the fact was stored as current in the database. 
Example: "HR entered the data on Feb 5." 
■Retroactive Change: Valid Time starts Jan 1, Transaction Time starts Feb 5. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2

563/1259 
Query Optimization 
Topic 10 
Query Optimization The Transformation Pipeline: Rewriting & Semantic Logic 
The Optimization Objective 
■Input: A declarative SQL query (The "What"). 
■ Output: An optimal Physical Execution Plan (The "How"). 
■The Challenge: SQL is high-level; the physical machinery (Disks, CPUs, Buffers) is low-level. 
■The Process: The DBMS must translate the SQL text into a tree of operators, and then find the most efficient version of that tree among potentially billions of equivalent alternatives. 
■Two Phases: 
1. Logical Optimization (Rewriting): Rule-based transformations that are always 
applied (Heuristics). 
2. Physical Optimization (Cost-Based): Comparing specific algorithms (e.g., Hash 
Join vs. Merge Join) based on data statistics. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
811/1259 
Query Optimization The Transformation Pipeline: Rewriting & Semantic Logic 
The Optimization Pipeline 
The lifecycle of a query before execution: 
Stage 
Parser 
Binder 
Rewriter 
Optimizer 
Responsibility 
Validates syntax and builds the initial Abstract Syntax Tree (AST). 
Semantic Analysis. Looks up System Catalogs to resolve names (e.g., "Does table T exist?", "Is column A an integer?"). 
Logical Optimization. Transforms the AST using algebraic rules (e.g., Push-down, Unnesting). (Topic of this section). 
Cost-Based Optimization (CBO). Generates physical plans and selects the cheapest one.

Query Optimization The Transformation Pipeline: Rewriting & Semantic Logic 
Parser & Semantic Analysis 
■ Syntax Check: Ensures the SQL grammar is correct. 
■Binding (Semantic Check): 
■Maps table names to internal Object IDs (OIDs). 
■ Verifies user permissions (ACLs). 
■Resolves ambiguous column names (e.g., if both joined tables have ID, which one is referenced?). 
■Type Checking: Ensures operators match data types (e.g., cannot divide a Date by a String). 
■Result: An Annotated Parse Tree where every node is linked to schema metadata.

813/1259 
Query Optimization The Transformation Pipeline: Rewriting & Semantic Logic 
The Logical Query Plan (Algebra Tree) 
■The Rewriter converts the Parse Tree into a Relational Algebra tree. 
■ Canonical Translation: A naive, direct mapping of the SFW block. 
Example (Naive Tree) 
Query: SELECT Name FROM Emp, Dept WHERE Emp.Did = Dept.id AND Salary > 50000 
1. Product (X): Cartesian Product of Emp and Dept. 
2. Filter (σ): Apply Emp.Did = Dept.id AND Salary > 50000. 
3. Project (π): Keep only Name. 
■ Problem: The Cartesian Product creates a massive intermediate result before filtering.

2025/26/2 
814/1259 
Query Optimization The Transformation Pipeline: Rewriting & Semantic Logic 
Rule-Based Optimization (Heuristics) 
■ Definition: Optimizations applied based on general rules that are generally known to improve performance, without examining data statistics. 
■ Principle: "Do less work." Reduce the data volume (cardinality) and width (tuples) as early as possible in the tree. 
■ Relational Equivalences: These transformations rely on the algebraic properties of operators (Commutativity, Associativity). 
■ Deterministic: If the pattern matches, the rule is applied. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
815/1259 
Query Optimization The Transformation Pipeline: Rewriting & Semantic Logic 
Heuristic 1: Predicate Push-down 
■Concept: Move Selection (o) operators down the tree, past Joins and Cross Products. 
■Goal: Filter rows before they are joined. This drastically reduces the size of the intermediate relation passed to the expensive Join operator. 
■ Equivalence: 
Op (RXS) = σp (R) × S 
(Assuming predicate P only refers to attributes of R). 
Applying Salary > 50k on Emp before joining with Dept saves I/O and CPU.

817/1259 
Query Optimization The Transformation Pipeline: Rewriting & Semantic Logic 
Heuristic 2: Projection Push-down 
■Concept: Move Projection (π) operators down the tree. 
■Goal: Reduce the "width" of tuples early. Eliminate attributes that are not needed for the final result or for join conditions. 
■ Impact: 
■ Memory: More tuples fit in the Buffer Pool / Cache. 
■1/0: Column-stores can skip reading unused columns entirely. 
■Network: Less data transfer in distributed systems. 
■Rule: If query asks for SELECT Name, we don't need to carry Address, Phone, Bio up the 
tree.

818/1259 
Expression Simplification 
Query Optimization The Transformation Pipeline: Rewriting & Semantic Logic 
■Constant Folding: Evaluating constant expressions at compile time. 
■ WHERE salary> 5000 * 12 → WHERE salary > 60000. 
■ WHERE date < NOW() - INTERVAL '1 DAY'→ Calculate timestamp once. 
■ Boolean Simplification: Removing tautologies and contradictions. 
■ WHERE 1=1 AND Price > 10 → WHERE Price > 10. 
☐ WHERE Price > 100 AND Price > 50→ WHERE Price > 100 (Subsumption). 
■Why? Reduces CPU instructions per row during scanning.

Query Optimization The Transformation Pipeline: Rewriting & Semantic Logic 
Subquery Unnesting (Flattening) 
■The Problem: Subqueries, especially correlated ones, are often executed as a "Nested Loop" in naive plans. Optimizers struggle to reorder operators across subquery boundaries. 
■ Goal: Rewrite subqueries into standard Joins (Inner, Left, or Semi). 
■ Benefit: Once flattened, the Cost-Based Optimizer can choose the best Join Algorithm (Hash vs. Merge) and Join Order. 
Example (Example: to 
} 
SELECT * FROM A WHERE ID IN (SELECT ID FROM B) 
↓ 
SELECT A.* FROM A JOIN B ON A.ID = B.ID (Semi-Join logic)

) 
■Context: Correlated subqueries reference values from the outer query. 
■ Naive Execution: Execute the inner query N times (once per outer row). Cost: N x M. 
■ Transformation: Pull the correlation up into a Join predicate. 
Example (Correlated Aggregation) 
SELECT * FROM Emp E WHERE Salary > ( 
SELECT AVG(Salary) FROM Emp E2 WHERE E2.Dept = E.Dept 
■Rewritten: Calculate AVG(Salary), GROUP BY Dept (Derived Table), then JOIN with Emp. 
Cost: 0(N).

821/1259 
Query Optimization The Transformation Pipeline: Rewriting & Semantic Logic 
Semantic Query Optimization (SQO) 
■Concept: Using the schema's integrity constraints (metadata) to infer simplifications 
that algebraic rules cannot see. 
■Inputs: Primary Keys, Foreign Keys, Check Constraints, NOT NULL. 
■ Join Elimination: 
■ Query: SELECT O.Date FROM Orders O JOIN Customers C ON O.C_ID = C.ID 
■Constraint: O.C_ID is a foreign key to C.ID and is NOT NULL. 
■Inference: The join cannot filter rows (every order matches a customer) and cannot duplicate rows (1:N). 
■ Action: Remove Customers table from the plan entirely. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
822/1259 
Query Optimization The Transformation Pipeline: Rewriting & Semantic Logic 
SQO: Contradiction Detection 
■ Pruning via Constraints: If a predicate contradicts a known constraint, the result is guaranteed to be empty. 
Example (Scenario) 
Schema: CREATE TABLE Products (... CHECK (Price > 0)) 
Query: SELECT * FROM Products WHERE Price = -5 
■Logical Inference: (Price > 0) Ʌ (Price = -5)→ False. 
■ Optimization: Replace the entire plan with a "Result: Empty Set" operator. 
■ Benefit: Zero 1/0. No locks acquired.

823/1259 
View Substitution 
Query Optimization The Transformation Pipeline: Rewriting & Semantic Logic 
■Standard Views: Virtual relations defined by a query. 
■Rewriting Process: 
1. Parser identifies a table name in FROM as a View. 
2. Rewriter retrieves the view definition from the Catalog. 
3. Substitutes the View's query tree into the main query tree (Macro expansion). 
■Merging: The optimizer then tries to merge the view's operators with the main query (e.g., pushing a filter from the main query into the view definition).

824/1259 
Query Optimization The Transformation Pipeline: Rewriting & Semantic Logic 
Materialized View Rewriting 
■ Advanced Feature: Automatic redirection to pre-computed results. 
■ Scenario: 
■ User queries the huge Sales table for sums by Region. 
■ A Materialized View MV_Sales_Region already exists with this data. 
■The Rewriter's Job: 
■Detect that the query is a subset of the Materialized View definition. 
■Rewrite the plan to scan MV_Sales_Region instead of Sales. 
■ Benefit: Massive performance gain transparent to the user (User does not need to know the MV exists).

Query Optimization The Transformation Pipeline: Rewriting & Semantic Logic 
Relational Equivalences: Join Properties 
■The foundation of the Search Space lies in the algebraic properties of Join. 
■Commutativity: R S = S 
R. 
■ Allows the optimizer to swap Inner/Outer tables (important for Hash Join build side). 
■ Associativity: (R = S) × T = R (ST). 
■ Allows the optimizer to change the order of joining (A → B → C vs B → C → A). 
■These properties create the combinatorial explosion of possible plans (N!) that the Cost-Based Optimizer must navigate.

826/1259 
Query Optimization The Cost Model: Measuring the Physical Reality 
The Cost-Based Optimizer (CB0) 
■The Goal: To select the execution plan with the lowest estimated "Cost" from the search space of valid plans. 
■ Cost Time: The CBO does not predict exact execution time (milliseconds). It calculates a dimensionless unit of work. 
■ The Equation: 
Cost ≈ 
(Disk 1/0) + (CPU Instructions) + (Memory Overhead) 
■The Dominant Factor: In traditional disk-oriented databases, Disk I/0 is weighted significantly higher than CPU operations due to the latency gap.

828/1259 
Query Optimization The Cost Model: Measuring the Physical Reality 
The Statistics Engine (System Catalog) 
■To estimate costs without running the query, the DBMS maintains metadata about the data distribution. 
■ Key Metrics Stored per Table (R) and Column (A): 
1. NR (Cardinality): Total number of tuples in relation R. 
2. V(A, R) (Distinct Values): The number of unique values in column A. 
3. Min/Max: The range of values (for integers/dates). 
4. Null Fraction: Percentage of rows where A is NULL. 
■ Maintenance: Statistics are updated via ANALYZE commands or auto-vacuum daemons. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
829/1259 
Query Optimization The Cost Model: Measuring the Physical Reality 
Selectivity (sel) 
■ Definition: The fraction of tuples in a relation that satisfy a specific predicate P. 
0≤ sel(P) ≤ 1 
■ Estimated Result Size: NP(R) = NR X sel(P). 
■ Importance: Selectivity determines: 
■ Whether to use an index (High selectivity / Low fraction). 
■The order of joins (Join most selective tables first). 
■ Memory grants for sorting/hashing.

■ Predicate: WHERE A = constant 
■ Assumption: Uniform Distribution. Every distinct value appears with equal

■Table Users, N = 1, 000, 000. 
■ Column Gender, V = 2 ('M', 'F').

831/1259 
Query Optimization The Cost Model: Measuring the Physical Reality 
Estimation Logic: Range Queries 
■ Predicate: WHERE A > c 
■ Assumption: Uniform distribution within the range [min(A), max(A)]. 
■ Formula: 
sel(A > c) = 
max(A) — c max(A) — min(A) 
■ Example: 
■ Column Price, Min=0, Max=100. 
■ Query: WHERE Price > 90. 
■sel = (100 - 90)/(100 – 0) = 0.1. 
■Edge Case: If c is outside the min/max bounds, sel = 0.

832/1259 
Query Optimization The Cost Model: Measuring the Physical Reality 
The Uniformity Myth & Data Skew 
■ Real-world data is rarely uniform (Zipfian distributions, Power laws). 
■Scenario: An e-commerce database. 
■ N = 1,000,000. 
■ Column City. V(City) = 1,000. 
■ Naive Est: sel = 1/1000 = 0.001 (1,000 rows per city). 
■Reality: 
■'New York': 500,000 rows. 
'Smallville': 10 rows. 
■ Consequence: The optimizer will underestimate the cost for 'New York' (choosing an Index Scan when a Table Scan was better) and overestimate for 'Smallville'. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
833/1259 
Query Optimization The Cost Model: Measuring the Physical Reality 
Histograms: Modeling Distribution 
■To handle skew, the DBMS stores a Histogram of the column values. 
■ A Histogram partitions the domain of values into B buckets. 
■Type 1: Equi-width Histograms 
■ Divide the value range (min, max) into equal-sized intervals. 
■Store the count of tuples in each interval. 
Example (Equi-width (Range 0-100, 4 Buckets)) 
■ Bucket 1 [0-25]: Count 10 
■ Bucket 2 [26-50]: Count 20 
■ Bucket 3 [51-75]: Count 900 
■ Bucket 4 [76-100]: Count 70 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
834/1259 
The Failure of Equi-width 
Query Optimization The Cost Model: Measuring the Physical Reality 
■ Problem: If the data is highly skewed, one bucket might contain 90% of the data. 
■ Within that "heavy" bucket, the optimizer still assumes uniformity. 
■ We lose resolution where the data is dense. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
835/1259 
Query Optimization The Cost Model: Measuring the Physical Reality 
Type 2: Equi-depth (Equi-height) Histograms 
■ Concept: Adjust the bucket boundaries so that each bucket contains roughly the same number of tuples (total rows / B). 
■ Storage: We store the Boundaries (Quantiles), not the counts (since counts are implicitly equal). 
■Benefit: High resolution in dense areas, low resolution in sparse areas. 
Example (Equi-depth (1000 rows, 4 Buckets → 250 rows/bucket)) 
■ Bucket 1: [0-18] (Contains 250 rows) 
■ Bucket 2: [1922] (Contains 250 rows) 
■ Bucket 3: [23 - 60] (Contains 250 rows) 
■ Bucket 4: [61 - 100] (Contains 250 rows)

841/1259 
Query Optimization The Cost Model: Measuring the Physical Reality 
Most Common Values (MCV) 
■ Hybrid Approach: Modern optimizers use a combination. 
■ 1. MCV List: Identify the top-k most frequent values. Store them and their exact frequencies explicitly. 
■ 2. Histogram: Build an equi-depth histogram on the remaining data (excluding the MCVs). 
■ Benefit: Removes the "outliers" that distort histogram boundaries, providing near-perfect estimation for heavy hitters (e.g., active status).

Complex Predicates: Conjunction 
■ Predicate: WHERE P1 AND P2 
■The Independence Assumption: The CBO assumes attributes are statistically 
independent. 
■ Formula: 
■ Example: 
sel(P1 ^ P2) = sel(P1) × sel(P2) 
■ sel(Make = 'Honda') = 0.1 
■ sel(Color = 'Red') = 0.2 
■ sel(Honda Ʌ Red) = 0.1 x 0.2 = 0.02.

843/1259 
The Correlation Trap 
Query Optimization The Cost Model: Measuring the Physical Reality 
■ Problem: Attributes are often highly correlated. 
■ Query: WHERE City = 'Paris' AND Country = 'France'. 
■sel('Paris')≈ 0.001. 
■sel('France')≈ 0.1. 
■ Independence Est: 0.001 x 0.1 
0.0001. 
■Reality: If City is Paris, Country is always France. The selectivity should be 0.001 (same as Paris). 
■ Error: The CBO drastically underestimates the result size (by order(s) of magnitude). 
■ Impact: Might choose a Nested Loop Join assuming 1 row returns, when actually 10,000 return, causing a performance cliff.

844/1259 
Query Optimization The Cost Model: Measuring the Physical Reality 
Solving Correlation: Multi-Column Statistics 
■ Solution: Allow the DBA to define statistics on groups of columns. 
■ Extended Stats (PostgreSQL): 
■ CREATE STATISTICS S ON City, Country FROM Locations; 
■ Mechanism: The DB calculates the functional dependency strength (City 
→ Country) or builds a multi-dimensional histogram. 
■Result: sel(Paris A France)≈ sel(Paris).

Disjunction and Negation 
Query Optimization The Cost Model: Measuring the Physical Reality 
■ Disjunction (OR): 
sel(P1 V P2) = sel(P1) + sel(P2) — sel(P1 ^ P2) 
Negation (NOT): 
sel(¬P1) = 1-sel(P1) 
■Note: If P1 is highly selective (sel = 0.01), P1 is highly unselective (0.99). Indices are rarely used for Negation.

Query Optimization The Cost Model: Measuring the Physical Reality 
Join Cardinality Estimation 
■ How many rows will RS return? 
■ Assumption: Uniformity and Containment (The join keys of the smaller set are a subset of the larger set). 
■ Formula (Join on Key A): 
NRÁS 
= 
NR X NS max(V (A, R), V(A,S)) 
■Logic: 
■This is essentially taking the Cartesian Product (NR X Ns) and reducing it by the selectivity of the join predicate (1/max(V)). 
■If joining PK to FK, V(PK) = NPK, simplifying the formula.

847/1259 
Query Optimization The Cost Model: Measuring the Physical Reality 
Cost Calculation Example: Scan vs. Index 
■ Scenario: Table T (106 pages). Query returns 1% of rows. 
■ Plan A: Sequential Scan 
■ Cost = 106 x 1.0 = 1,000,000 units. 
■ Plan B: Unclustered Index Scan 
■ Pages accessed≈ 1% of 106 = 10,000 pages. 
■ Cost = 10,000 × 4.0 (Random Factor) = 40, 000 units. 
■ Decision: Plan B is cheaper. 
■Drift: If selectivity rises to 30%, Plan B cost becomes 300,000 x 4 = 1,200,000. Plan A becomes cheaper.

848/1259 
Query Optimization The Cost Model: Measuring the Physical Reality 
Plan Drift and Stale Statistics 
■ Plan Drift: When the optimal execution plan changes due to changes in data distribution. 
■Stale Stats: If NR grows from 1,000 to 1,000,000 but ANALYZE is not run: 
■CBO thinks table is small. 
■Chooses Nested Loop Join (Efficient for small N, catastrophic for large N). 
■ Query hangs. 
■Robustness: Modern CBOs sometimes choose slightly sub-optimal but "safer" plans (e.g., Hash Join) to avoid the worst-case scenario of Nested Loops on unexpected large data.

849/1259 
Query Optimization The Search Space: Join Ordering & Tree Shapes 
The Combinatorial Explosion 
■The Problem: For a query joining N tables, in what order should we join them? 
■ Algebraic Freedom: Thanks to the Associative and Commutative properties of Join: 
■ Search Space Size: 
(A × B) × C = A × (BC) = (CA) B 
■ For N relations, the number of possible join orders is roughly proportional to 4N. 
■ Even restricting to Left-Deep Trees, there are N! permutations. 
■Complexity: Join Ordering is NP-Hard.

■ Implication: We cannot exhaustively search every possible plan for complex queries. 
■ Strategy: 
1. Restrict the shape of the tree (e.g., only consider Left-Deep). 
2. Use Dynamic Programming to prune sub-optimal paths early.

851/1259 
Query Optimization The Search Space: Join Ordering & Tree Shapes 
Tree Shape: Left-Deep Trees 
■Structure: The right-hand child of every join is a Base Table. 
■ Visual: A linear chain. ((AB) α C) × D. 
■ Advantages: 
■ Pipelining: Fits the Iterator Model perfectly. The result of a join is immediately fed into the next join. 
■Resource Management: We only need to keep one "Build" side in memory at a time (for Hash Joins). 
■Search Space: Reduced to N! permutations. 
■ Industry Standard: Used by the original System R and many modern optimizers as the default heuristic. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
852/1259 
Tree Shape: Bushy Trees 
Query Optimization The Search Space: Join Ordering & Tree Shapes 
■ Structure: The children of a join can be Intermediate Results (Result of another join). 
■ Visual: (A ▷ B) × (C × D). 
■ Advantages: 
■ Parallelism: A B and C D can be computed independently on separate cores/nodes. 
■ Intermediate Size: Sometimes joining small tables C and D first reduces the intermediate result size before joining with the massive A ▷ B result. 
■ Disadvantage: Massive search space explosion. Requires materialization of intermediate temporary tables.

854/1259 
Query Optimization The Search Space: Join Ordering & Tree Shapes 
Star Schema Optimization 
■ Context: Data Warehousing. One huge Fact Table (F) joined with many small Dimension Tables (D1, D2, D3). 
■ Shape: Usually results in a Left-Deep tree where the Fact Table is the base, and dimensions are joined one by one. 
■ Optimization: 
■ Star-Join Transformation: Cartesian product of Dimensions (D1 X D2) might be small enough to compute first, then join to F via a multi-column index. 
■ Bitmap Join Indices: Pre-computed indices linking Dimension values directly to Fact rows.

2025/26/2 
855/1259 
The Selinger Algorithm 
Query Optimization Dynamic Programming (The Selinger Algorithm) 
■ Origin: IBM System R (1979). The foundation of modern CBOs. 
■ Core Idea: Use Dynamic Programming to build the optimal plan bottom-up. 
■ Principle of Optimality: 
■To find the optimal plan for joining N tables, we must combine optimal plans for subsets of those tables. 
■ Example: The best way to join (A, B, C) involves finding the best way to join (A, B) and then joining C, or (A, C) then B, etc. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
856/1259 
Query Optimization Dynamic Programming (The Selinger Algorithm) 
Step 1: Access Paths (Single Relation) 
■Input: All tables in the query {R1, R2, ..., Rn}. 
■ Action: For each table, calculate the cost of every possible access method. 
1. Sequential Scan: Cost≈ Npages" 
2. Index Scan (Index 1): Cost≈ Traversal + Selectivity. 
3. Index Scan (Index 2): ... 
■ Pruning: Discard expensive paths, unless they provide an Interesting Order (see later). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
857/1259 
Query Optimization Dynamic Programming (The Selinger Algorithm) 
Step 2: Pairs (2-Way Joins) 
■Input: The set of optimal single-table plans. 
■ Action: Consider all pairs {R, R;} where a join condition exists. 
■Calculations: 
■Try Nested Loop: R; (Outer) ▷ R; (Inner). 
■Try Hash Join: Build Ri, Probe Rj. 
■Try Sort-Merge: Sort R, Sort R, Merge. 
■ Selection: For the set {A, B}, keep the cheapest plan (e.g., Hash Join A ▷ B). Store cost and cardinality. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
858/1259 
Query Optimization Dynamic Programming (The Selinger Algorithm) 
Step 3: Inductive Step (k-Way Joins) 
■ Input: Optimal plans for subsets of size k - 1. 
■ Action: To find optimal plan for subset of size k: 
■Combine optimal plan for subset S (size k - 1) with table T (size 1). 
■ ST. 
■Left-Deep Restriction: We do not consider combining subset S1 (size 2) with subset S2 (size 2) to make size 4. We only add one base table at a time.

860/1259 
Interesting Orders 
Query Optimization Dynamic Programming (The Selinger Algorithm) 
■The Pruning Problem: A plan might be more expensive locally but cheaper globally. 
■ Scenario: 
■ Plan 1 (Hash Join): Cost 100. Output: Unordered. 
■ Plan 2 (Sort-Merge): Cost 120. Output: Sorted by Join Key X. 
■Future Need: The next operator is GROUP BY X. 
■ Plan 1 requires sorting (100 + 50 = 150). 
■ Plan 2 requires no sorting (120 + 0 = 120). 
■Rule: Do not prune a more expensive plan if it produces an Interesting Order (an order useful for later Joins, Aggregates, or ORDER BY).

861/1259 
Query Optimization Dynamic Programming (The Selinger Algorithm) 
Physical Operator Selection 
The optimizer chooses the physical algorithm based on cost formulas. 
Algorithm 
Nested Loop 
Hash Join 
Sort-Merge 
Selection Criteria 
One input is very small, or an index exists on the join column of the inner table. 
Standard for large, unsorted inputs. Requires memory for build table. 
Inputs are already sorted (Interesting Order) or inputs are massive and memory is constrained (spills gracefully).

862/1259 
Query Optimization Dynamic Programming (The Selinger Algorithm) 
Pruning the Search Space 
■ Even with DP, the space is large. Modern optimizers use heuristics to prune further. 
■ Cartesian Products: Defer cross-products (joins without predicates) as late as possible. 
■ Plan Cost Threshold: If a plan exceeds the cost of the "Best Plan So Far" during construction, stop exploring that branch. 
■ Genetic Algorithms: For queries with > 12 tables, switch from DP to randomized search (PostgreSQL geqo).

■Total: 180. Output: Ordered by x.

Query Optimization Dynamic Programming (The Selinger Algorithm) 
■We have three candidates for {A, B}:

■ Pruning Decision: 
■ Hash (120) is dominated by NLJ (60). Discard Hash. 
■ SMJ (180) is expensive, but preserves interesting order (A.x). Keep SMJ. 
■Result: Store NLJ as best generic plan, SMJ as best ordered plan.

■Left-Deep Rule: We treat the Level 2 result as the "Outer" relation (usually) to pipeline execution.

■ Plan A (Hash + Sort): 410. 
■ Plan B (Preserve Order): 450. 
☐ Winner: Plan A. 
■ Why? The benefit of preserving the index order from Table A was lost due to the high cost of maintaining it through the joins (SMJ/NLJ) compared to the efficiency of Hash Joins. 
■ Outcome: The "Interesting Order" was pruned at the very last step.

877/1259 
Query Optimization Dynamic Programming (The Selinger Algorithm) 
Selinger Algorithm Summary 
1. Enumeration: Generate valid join orders (usually Left-Deep). 2. Costing: Estimate cardinality and I/O cost for each step. 
3. Pruning: Keep the cheapest plan for each subset of tables... 
4. Exception: ...PLUS any plans that produce an Interesting Order. 
5. Output: The physical plan tree corresponding to the cheapest path at the top level. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
878/1259 
The Execution Plan 
Query Optimization Plan Management & Observability 
■ Definition: The specific tree of physical operators (algorithms) selected by the optimizer to execute a query. 
■ Observability: The EXPLAIN command reveals this internal decision process. 
■ Key Metrics Exposed: 
■ Estimated Cost: Arbitrary units (e.g., disk page fetches). 
■ Estimated Rows: Cardinality prediction. 
■ Access Path: Sequential Scan vs. Index Scan. 
■ Join Method: Nested Loop, Hash, or Merge.

879/1259 
Query Optimization Plan Management & Observability 
Interpreting EXPLAIN Output 
■Most engines display plans as indented text representing a tree structure (Bottom-up execution). 
Example (PostgreSQL Style Output) 
Hash Join (cost=40.00..85.00 rows=100 width=16) 
Hash Cond: (orders.c_id = customer.id) 
-> Seq Scan on orders (cost-0.00..30.00 rows=1000...) 
-> Hash (cost=20.00..20.00 rows=50...) 
-> Seq Scan on customer (cost-0.00..20.00 ...) 
Filter: (region = 'West') 
■Indentation: The Hash node is a child of Hash Join. 
■ Startup Cost (40.00): Time before the first row is emitted (waiting for Hash Build). 
■Total Cost (85.00): Time until the last row is emitted. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
880/1259 
Query Optimization Plan Management & Observability 
Plan Stability: Parameter Sniffing 
■The Mechanism: When a query with parameters (WHERE status = ?) is compiled, the optimizer "sniffs" the specific parameter value to choose a plan. 
■The Problem: Data Skew. 
■Case A ('Active'): 99% of rows. Optimal Plan: Table Scan. 
■Case B ('Banned'): 1% of rows. Optimal Plan: Index Seek. 
■The Disaster: 
1. Query compiles first with 'Banned'. Plan (Index Seek) is cached. 
2. Later, query runs with 'Active'. Reuses cached Index Seek plan. 
3. Performance Cliff: Doing 1,000,000 random index lookups is far slower than 1 
sequential scan. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
881/1259 
Plan Drift & Regressions 
Query Optimization Plan Management & Observability 
■ Plan Drift: When the query plan changes over time due to environmental changes. 
■ Causes: 
■ Statistics Updates: Auto-analyze runs and changes cardinality estimates slightly, tipping the cost model from Hash Join to Nested Loop. 
■ Schema Changes: Adding/Dropping an index. 
■ Software Upgrades: New optimizer version changes cost constants. 
■ Plan Regression: When a new plan is significantly worse than the old one. 
■ Mitigation: Plan Pinning / Baselines (forcing the DBMS to use a known good plan). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2

■Use Case: When the DBA knows the data distribution better than the CBO (e.g., correlated columns, stale stats).

Query Optimization Plan Management & Observability 
Plan Caching: Prepared Statements 
■ Hard Parse: Parsing + Optimization + Code Gen. High CPU cost. 
■Soft Parse: Looking up a pre-compiled plan in the Plan Cache by query hash. 
■ Prepared Statement Lifecycle: 
1. PREPARE stmt(int) AS SELECT * FROM t WHERE id = $1 
2. Optimize: Plan generated (generic or custom). 
3. EXECUTE stmt(100) 
4. EXECUTE stmt(200) (Reuses plan). 
■ Trade-off: Saves optimization time (0(ms)) but risks Parameter Sniffing issues.

885/1259 
Query Optimization Plan Management & Observability 
Advanced: Optimizing Window Functions 
■The Bottleneck: Window functions require Sort operations (PARTITION BY X ORDER BY Y). 
■ Optimization 1: Index Utilization 
■If an index exists on (X, Y), the Sort operator is removed. 
■ Optimization 2: Common Partition Elimination 
■ Query: RANK() OVER (P_A), SUM() OVER (P_A) 
■The engine sorts by PA once and computes both functions in a single pass. 
■ Optimization 3: Frame Reduction 
■ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING can be computed incrementally (running total). 
■ROWS BETWEEN 5 PRECEDING requires an Inverse function (add new, subtract old) to be 0(1) rather than O(N).

2025/26/2 
886/1259 
Query Optimization Plan Management & Observability 
Summary of Query Optimization 
1. Rewrite: Transform SQL into a more efficient logical algebra (Push-down, 
Unnesting). 
2. Estimate: Use Statistics (Histograms, MCVs) to guess cardinality and selectivity. 
3. Search: Use Dynamic Programming (Selinger) to explore Join Orders and Algorithms. 4. Cost: Pick the plan with the lowest estimated resource usage (10 + CPU). 
5. Execute: Run the physical plan (Volcano Model) and manage runtime resources 
(Memory Grants).
