Concurrency & Recovery 
Topic 11 
Concurrency & Recovery Transaction Theory: The ACID Formalism 
The Logical Unit of Work 
■ Definition: A Transaction (T) is a sequence of operations (reads and writes) that forms a single logical unit of data processing. 
■Boundaries: 
■BEGIN: Starts the transaction boundary. 
■ COMMIT: Successful termination. All changes are made permanent. 
■ ROLLBACK (ABORT): Unsuccessful termination. All changes are undone. 
■ The Abstraction: The DBMS provides the illusion that the transaction executes on a dedicated machine, sequentially, without partial failure. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
889/1259 
The ACID Framework 
Concurrency & Recovery Transaction Theory: The ACID Formalism 
Proposed by Jim Gray and Reuter (1983) to define the safety guarantees of a transaction processing system. 
Property 
Atomicity 
Consistency 
Isolation 
Durability 
Guarantee 
"All or nothing." No partial execution effects. 
Transitions from one valid state to another. 
Concurrent execution results equal sequential execution. 
Committed changes survive system failures. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
890/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Transaction Theory: The ACID Formalism 
Atomicity: The Undo Rule 
■Scenario: A transaction transfers $100 from Account A to Account B. 
■ Steps: 
1. Read A ($1000). 
2. Write A ($900). 
3. CRASH! (Power failure). 
4. (Step 4: Write B ($1100) never happens). 
■ Violation: Money has vanished. 
■Guarantee: Upon restart, the DBMS must detect the incomplete transaction and Undo (Rollback) the write to A, restoring it to $1000. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
891/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Transaction Theory: The ACID Formalism 
Consistency: Logical Correctness 
■ Definition: The database must satisfy all defined integrity constraints (Primary Keys, Foreign Keys, CHECK) before and after the transaction. 
■ Responsibility Split: 
■ DBMS: Enforces defined constraints. 
■ Application: Ensures business logic correctness (e.g., "Total assets = Total liabilities"). 
■ Transient Inconsistency: During execution, the DB may be temporarily inconsistent (e.g., A is debited, B is not yet credited), provided consistency is restored by COMMIT. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
892/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Transaction Theory: The ACID Formalism 
Isolation: The Concurrency Illusion 
■ Scenario: Two users run transactions T1 and T1⁄2 simultaneously. 
■ Problem: Interleaved operations can lead to race conditions. 
■ Serializability: The result of any concurrent execution must be equivalent to some serial execution order (T1→ T2 or T2 → T1). 
■ Mechanism: Locking protocols (2PL) or Multi-Version Concurrency Control (MVCC). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
893/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Transaction Theory: The ACID Formalism 
Durability: The Redo Rule 
■ Definition: Once a user receives a "Success" message for a COMMIT, the changes must persist even if the server catches fire immediately after. 
■The Physical Bottleneck: Writing to data pages (Random I/O) is slow. 
■ Solution: Write-Ahead Logging (WAL). 
■ Protocol: The log record (Sequential I/O) representing the change must reach stable storage before the transaction is acknowledged committed. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
894/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Transaction Theory: The ACID Formalism 
Formalizing Concurrency: Schedules 
■ Schedule (S): A sequence of operations from a set of transactions. 
■ Serial Schedule: Operations of different transactions do not interleave. (T1 finishes, then T2 starts). 
■Concurrent Schedule: Operations are interleaved. 
■Goal: To allow concurrent schedules (for performance) that are Equivalent to serial schedules (for correctness). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
895/1259 
Conflict Operations 
Concurrency & Recovery Transaction Theory: The ACID Formalism 
■ Two operations O; and 0; from different transactions T and T; conflict if: 
1. They access the same data item X. 
2. At least one of them is a Write. 
■Conflict Types: 
■ Read-Write (RW): Unrepeatable Read. T; reads X, then T; writes X. 
■ Write-Read (WR): Dirty Read. T; writes X, then Tj; reads X. 
■ Write-Write (WW): Lost Update. T; writes X, then T; writes X. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
896/1259 
Conflict Serializability 
Concurrency & Recovery Transaction Theory: The ACID Formalism 
■Conflict Equivalent: Two schedules S1 and S2 are equivalent if they involve the same operations and every pair of conflicting operations is ordered the same way in both. 
■Conflict Serializable: A schedule S is conflict serializable if it is conflict equivalent to some serial schedule. 
■This is the standard correctness criterion used by commercial databases. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
897/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Transaction Theory: The ACID Formalism 
The Precedence Graph (Serialization Graph) 
■ A directed graph G = (V,E) used to test for serializability. 
■ Nodes (V): Transactions {T1, T2, ..., Tn}. 
■ Edges (E): Draw edge T; → T; if an operation in T; conflicts with and precedes an operation in Tj. 
Theorem (Conflict Serializability Theorem) 
A schedule is Conflict Serializable if and only if its Precedence Graph is Acyclic. 
■If acyclic, a topological sort of the graph yields the equivalent serial order. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
898/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Transaction Theory: The ACID Formalism 
Testing Serializability: Example 
■ Schedule: R1(A), W2(A), R1 (B), W2(B). 
■ Conflicts: 
1. R1(A) vs W2(A) (RW conflict on A). Order: T1 → T2- 2. R1(B) vs W2(B) (RW conflict on B). Order: T1 → T2. 
■Graph: T1→T2. No cycles. 
■Result: Serializable. Equivalent to Serial Order (T1, T2).

899/1259 
The Cycle (Anomaly) 
Concurrency & Recovery Transaction Theory: The ACID Formalism 
■ Schedule: R1(A), W1⁄2 (A), R2(B), W1(B). 
■ 
Conflicts: 
1. R1(A) before W2(A): Edge T1 → T2- 
2. R2(B) before W1(B): Edge T1⁄2 → T1. 
■Graph: T1T2. 
■Result: Cycle Detected. Not Conflict Serializable. 
■Meaning: Neither (T1, T2) nor (T2, T1) can produce this outcome. The database state 
is potentially corrupted.

901/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Transaction Theory: The ACID Formalism 
Anomaly Taxonomy: Dirty Read 
■ Definition: T1 reads data written by T2 that has not yet committed. 
■Risk: If T2 rolls back, T1 has processed data that never effectively existed ("reading 
the future that failed"). 
■SQL Level: Permitted only in READ UNCOMMITTED. 
■ Prevention: Use shared locks (2PL) or read only committed versions (MVCC). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
902/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Transaction Theory: The ACID Formalism 
Anomaly Taxonomy: Non-Repeatable Read 
■ Definition: T1 reads row X. T2 updates row X and commits. T1 reads row X again and sees a different value. 
■Risk: Inconsistent analysis within a single transaction logic. 
■SQL Level: Prevented by REPEATABLE READ. 
■ Prevention: Hold shared locks until end of transaction (strict 2PL).

2025/26/2 
903/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Transaction Theory: The ACID Formalism 
Anomaly Taxonomy: Phantoms 
■ Definition: T1 runs a range query (WHERE Age > 10) and gets set S1. T2 inserts a new row that satisfies the predicate. T1 runs the query again and gets S2 (where 
|S2| > |S1|). 
■ Distinction: Non-repeatable read affects existing rows. Phantoms arise from new rows appearing. 
■ SQL Level: Prevented only by SERIALIZABLE. 
■ Prevention: Predicate Locks, Gap Locks, or Range Locks.

908/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Transaction Theory: The ACID Formalism 
Preventing Phantoms: Next-Key Locking 
■Solution: In SERIALIZABLE (via Index Locking). 
■ Mechanism: Lock the index entry and the gap before it. 
■Trace with Gap Locks: 
1. T1 reads Age > 10. 
2. T1 locks Row (20) and Gap (10, 20]. 
3. T1 locks Row (25) and Gap (20, 25]. 
4. T1 locks Gap (25,∞). 
5. T2 tries INSERT (3, 15). 15 falls in Gap (10, 20]. 6. Block: T2 waits for T1 to release the Gap Lock.

The Lock Manager 
Concurrency & Recovery Pessimistic Concurrency: Locking & Latches 
■Concept: A centralized module in the DBMS that manages access to database objects (Tuples, Pages, Tables). 
■ Protocol: Transactions must request a lock before accessing an object and release it when done. 
■Lock Types: 
■ Shared (S-Lock): Required for reading. 
■ Exclusive (X-Lock): Required for writing. 
■The Compatibility Matrix: 
Request \Held

2025/26/2 
910/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Pessimistic Concurrency: Locking & Latches 
Two-Phase Locking (2PL) 
■The Theorem: If all transactions follow the 2PL protocol, the resulting schedule is guaranteed to be Conflict Serializable. 
■ Phase 1: Growing Phase 
■ Transaction requests locks (S or X) as needed. 
■The lock manager grants or blocks (waits). 
■Constraint: No locks can be released. 
■ Phase 2: Shrinking Phase 
■ Transaction releases locks. 
■Constraint: No new locks can be acquired.

■ Problem with Standard 2PL: Cascading Aborts. 
■ If T1 releases a lock on X, and T2 reads X, then T1 aborts... T2 read dirty data and must also abort.

Concurrency & Recovery Pessimistic Concurrency: Locking & Latches 
Strict Two-Phase Locking (S2PL) 
■The Standard Implementation: Used by almost all locking databases. 
■Rule: Hold all Exclusive (X) locks until the transaction terminates (COMMIT or ABORT). 
■ Benefit: Ensures Cascading Aborts never happen.

913/1259 
Deadlock Detection 
Concurrency & Recovery Pessimistic Concurrency: Locking & Latches 
■ Scenario: T1 holds Lock A, wants Lock B. T2 holds Lock B, wants Lock A. 
■State: Both wait forever. 
■ Detection Algorithm: The DBMS maintains a Wait-For Graph. 
■Nodes: Active Transactions. 
■ Edge Ti→ Tj: T; is waiting for a lock held by Tj. 
■ Action: A background thread periodically checks for cycles. 
■Resolution: Pick a "victim" (usually the youngest or least work done) and ABORT it. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
914/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Pessimistic Concurrency: Locking & Latches 
Deadlock Prevention (Timestamp Protocols) 
■ Assign timestamps (TS) to transactions at start time. 
■Wait-Die (Non-Preemptive): 
■If Older (TS; <TS;) requests lock held by Younger: Wait. 
■If Younger (TS; > TS;) requests lock held by Older: Die (Self-Abort). 
■ Wound-Wait (Preemptive): 
■ If Older requests lock held by Younger: Wound (Abort Younger). 
■If Younger requests lock held by Older: Wait. 
■Result: No cycles can form because edges only flow in one timestamp direction (e.g., Old → Young). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
915/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Pessimistic Concurrency: Locking & Latches 
Multi-Granularity Locking (MGL) 
■ Problem: How to lock a Table? Locking every row individually is inefficient (1 000 000 lock manager calls). 
■ Solution: Hierarchy of locks (Database → Table → Page → Row). 
■ Intent Locks: Signals that a transaction plans to lock nodes lower in the tree. 
Mode 
Meaning 
IS 
Intention Shared. "I want to read some rows below." 
IX 
Intention Exclusive. "I want to write some rows below." 
SIX 
Shared + Intention Exclusive. "I am reading the whole table, but will write specific rows."

■ Granularities: Database, Table, Page, Row. 
■Lock Modes: Shared (S), Exclusive (X), Intention Shared (IS), Intention Exclusive (IX), Shared + Intention Exclusive (SIX). 
■The same compatibility rules apply at every level of the hierarchy.

917/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Pessimistic Concurrency: Locking & Latches 
Locking Hierarchy Logic 
■ Protocol: 
1. To get S on a node, you must hold IS (or stronger) on parent. 2. To get X on a node, you must hold IX (or stronger) on parent. 3. Locks are released bottom-up. 
■ Example: Update Row 50 in Table T. 
■Lock DB (IX). 
■Lock Table T (IX). 
■Lock Page P (IX). 
■Lock Row 50 (X). 
■Conflict Check: If another transaction holds S on Table T, it conflicts with the IX request on T. The update waits at the table level.

2025/26/2 
918/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Pessimistic Concurrency: Locking & Latches 
Index Locking (Latches) 
■Locks vs. Latches: 
■Lock: Logical transaction protection (Long duration, Deadlock detection, Rollback support). 
■Latch: Physical data structure protection (Short duration, No deadlock detection). Equivalent to Mutex/Spinlock. 
■ B+ Tree Concurrency: 
■We need to prevent a Split operation (modifying tree structure) from interfering with a Reader. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
919/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Pessimistic Concurrency: Locking & Latches 
Latch Crabbing (Coupling) 
■ Search Protocol: 
1. Get Latch on Parent. 
2. Get Latch on Child. 
3. Release Latch on Parent if Child is "Safe". 
■Safe Node: A node that will not split (Insert) or merge (Delete). 
■ Visual: A reader traverses down the tree like a crab, holding two latches momentarily. 
■ Write Optimization: Optimistically assume leaf is safe (Read Latch down). If leaf is full, restart traversal with Write Latches.

920/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Pessimistic Concurrency: Locking & Latches 
B+ Tree Concurrency (Latches) 
■Goal: Traverse tree safely while others might insert/split. 
■ Protocol (Search): 
Acquire Latch on Parent. 
Acquire Latch on Child. 
■Safe? If Child is safe (won't split), release Parent.

■ Current held: {A}.

■Is B safe? Yes (not full). 
■Release A. 
■ Current held: {B}. 
Step 4: Leaf 
■Get Read-Latch on D. 
■Release B. 
Read D. Release D. 
"Crabbing" movement: Hold at least one anchor point to prevent the subtree from being invalidated above you.

922/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Modern Concurrency: Multi-Version (MVCC) 
Multi-Version Concurrency Control (MVCC) 
■ Core Philosophy: "Writers do not block Readers. Readers do not block Writers." 
■Mechanism: 
■ Updates do not overwrite data in place. 
■Instead, they create a new version of the tuple. 
■Reads conceptually travel back in time to see the version consistent with their start time. 
■Storage: Each tuple has metadata fields: 
■xmin: Transaction ID that created this version. 
■ xmax: Transaction ID that deleted (or updated) this version. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2

Concurrency & Recovery Modern Concurrency: Multi-Version (MVCC) 
MVCC Physical Layout (PostgreSQL Style) 
■ A logical tuple is a linked list of physical versions (02N: Oldest-to-Newest). 
■Current State: Row 100 has been updated twice.

2025/26/2 
924/1259 
MVCC Visibility Walk 
Concurrency & Recovery Modern Concurrency: Multi-Version (MVCC) 
■ Scenario: Transaction Tcurr with ID 75 reads the row. 
■Logic: Scan the chain until a visible version is found. 
1. Visit V3 (Latest): 
■xmin = 90. Is 90 ≤ 75? No. 
■ V3 is "in the future". Ignore. Follow back-pointer (or traverse from head). 
2. Visit V2: 
■xmin = 60. Is 60 ≤ 75? Yes. (Created in past). 
■xmax = 90. Is 90 committed? Yes. Is 90 > 75? Yes. 
■ V2 was deleted by Tx 90, but Tx 90 is "future" relative to us. Result: V2 is the visible version. Return Value: 20. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
925/1259 
MVCC Visibility Rules 
Concurrency & Recovery Modern Concurrency: Multi-Version (MVCC) 
■ A transaction T (with ID txid) can see a tuple version V if: 
1. V.xmin is committed and V.xmin < txid. (Created in the past). 
2. V.xmax is NULL (Active) OR V.xmax is not committed OR V.xmax > txid 
(Deleted in the future). 
■ Own Updates: T can always see versions where xmin = txid. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
926/1259 
Snapshot Isolation (SI) 
Concurrency & Recovery Modern Concurrency: Multi-Version (MVCC) 
■ Definition: Each transaction reads data from a consistent snapshot of the database taken at the start of the transaction. 
■ Write Conflict: If T1 and T2 try to update the same row concurrently: 
■T1 commits first. 
■T2 attempts to update. It sees that the row has changed since its snapshot started. 
■T2 Aborts (First-Committer-Wins rule). 
■ Prevents Dirty Read, Non-Repeatable Read, and Phantoms. 
■Does not guarantee Serializability (Write Skew). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
927/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Modern Concurrency: Multi-Version (MVCC) 
The Write Skew Anomaly 
Example (Scenario: Two Doctors) 
Constraint: At least one doctor must be on call. Current: Alice (On Call), Bob (On Call). 
■ T1 (Alice): Checks count (2). Goes off call (Update Alice). 
■T2 (Bob): Checks count (2). Goes off call (Update Bob). 
■Commit: Both commit successfully under Snapshot Isolation. 
■Result: Zero doctors on call. Constraint violated. 
■Reason: They read overlapping rows but updated disjoint rows based on the shared aggregate. No lock conflict occurred.

■ Initial State: Alice (true), Bob (true). 
■Constraint: SELECT COUNT(*) FROM Doctors WHERE OnCall = true must be > 1. 
■Isolation: SNAPSHOT (MVCC).

932/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Modern Concurrency: Multi-Version (MVCC) 
Serializable Snapshot Isolation (SSI) 
■ Goal: Achieve full Serializability with MVCC performance (no locking). 
■Mechanism: Track dependencies between transactions. 
■ rw-dependency: T1 reads a version that T2 overwrites (Anti-dependency). 
■ Cycle Detection: If there is a cycle of rw-dependencies, abort one transaction. 
■ Used by PostgreSQL to provide SERIALIZABLE isolation without locking the whole table.

936/1259 
The Goal of Recovery 
Concurrency & Recovery Recovery: The ARIES Algorithm 
■Scenario: The database system crashes (Power failure, OS Kernel Panic, Process Kill). 
■RAM State: The Buffer Pool contents (Dirty Pages) are lost instantly. 
■Disk State: Contains a mixture of: 
■Changes from committed transactions (Good). 
■ Missing changes from committed transactions (Bad - Durability violation). 
■ Changes from uncommitted transactions (Bad - Atomicity violation). 
■Goal: Restore the database to a consistent state where all committed transactions are present and all uncommitted ones are rolled back. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
937/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Recovery: The ARIES Algorithm 
The Storage Policy Matrix 
Recovery complexity depends on how the Buffer Manager interacts with Disk. 
Policy 
STEAL 
NO-STEAL 
FORCE 
NO-FORCE 
Definition 
Implication 
Can uncommitted pages be written Requires Undo (to reverse the to disk? (Yes) 
write if T aborts). 
Uncommitted pages stay in RAM. 
(No) 
Must pages be written to disk at Commit? (Yes) 
Pages can stay in RAM after Commit. (No) 
Low concurrency (RAM fills up). 
Requires High I/O (Random writes at Commit). 
Requires Redo (to replay the write if crash occurs). 
ARIES Choice: STEAL/NO-FORCE. (Best performance, hardest recovery).

2025/26/2 
938/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Recovery: The ARIES Algorithm 
Write-Ahead Logging (WAL) 
Any modification to a database page must be recorded in the Log on stable storage before the modified page is written to disk. 
■ Structure: The Log is a sequential append-only file of Log Records. 
■Log Record: (LSN, TransID, PageID, PrevLSN, Type, UndoInfo, RedoInfo). 
■LSN (Log Sequence Number): A monotonically increasing unique identifier for every log record. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
939/1259 
LSN Management 
Concurrency & Recovery Recovery: The ARIES Algorithm 
■pageLSN: Stored in the Page Header of every data page. It records the LSN of the last update to that page. 
■ flushedLSN: Stored in RAM. Tracks the highest LSN currently on Disk (Stable Log). 
■WAL Rule implementation: Before the Buffer Manager writes a dirty page P to disk: 
if (P.pageLSN > flushedLSN) FlushLog(); 
This ensures we never write data that we cannot describe (and thus undo). 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
940/1259 
Checkpoints (Fuzzy) 
Concurrency & Recovery Recovery: The ARIES Algorithm 
■ Problem: Without checkpoints, recovery must replay the log from the beginning of time. 
■ Action: 
1. Write a BEGIN_CHECKPOINT record. 
2. Capture the state of the Transaction Table (Active transactions). 
3. Capture the state of the Dirty Page Table (Modified pages in RAM). 
4. Write an END_CHECKPOINT record containing these tables. 
■Fuzzy: We do not flush dirty pages to disk. Normal processing continues during checkpointing. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
941/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Recovery: The ARIES Algorithm 
ARIES Phase 1: Analysis 
■Goal: Restore the Transaction Table (TT) and Dirty Page Table (DPT) to the state they 
were in at the moment of the crash. 
■Start: Read the Master Record to find the last BEGIN_CHECKPOINT. 
■ Process: Scan the Log forward from Checkpoint to End-of-Log. 
■Logic: 
■If Tx-Start found: Add to TT. 
■If Tx-Commit/End found: Remove from TT. 
■If Update record found on Page P: Add P to DPT (if not present) with recLSN = current LSN. 
■ Output: The set of "Loser" transactions (Active at crash) and "Potential Dirty Pages". 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
942/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Recovery: The ARIES Algorithm 
ARIES Phase 2: Redo (History Repeating) 
■ Goal: Replay all actions to restore the database to the exact state at the crash (including uncommitted changes). 
■Start Point: The smallest recLSN in the Dirty Page Table (The oldest dirty page). 
■ Process: Scan Log forward. 
■ Action: For each update record: 
1. Is Page P in DPT? 
2. Is LSN > recLSN (P)? 
3. Read Page P from disk. Is P.pageLSN < LSN? 
■ Execute: If checks pass, apply the change (Redo) and update P.pageLSN. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
943/1259 
ARIES Phase 3: Undo 
Concurrency & Recovery Recovery: The ARIES Algorithm 
■ Goal: Rollback all "Loser" transactions identified in Analysis (and not committed during Redo). 
■ Process: Scan Log backward starting from the end. 
■ Mechanism: 
■Maintain a set ToUndo containing the last LSN of every loser transaction. 
■ Pick the largest LSN from ToUndo. 
■ Undo that action. 
■ Write a Compensation Log Record (CLR) to the log (Redo-only record describing the undo). 
■ Add PrevLSN of the undone record to ToUndo. 
■Termination: When ToUndo is empty. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
944/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Recovery: The ARIES Algorithm 
Compensation Log Records (CLRs) 
■ The Problem: What if the system crashes during the Undo phase? 
■Risk: We might try to undo the same action twice, or undo an undo. 
■The Solution: CLRs. 
■ A CLR records that "I have undone LSN X." 
■It contains a pointer UndoNextLSN which points to the record before the one we just undid. 
■ Effect: During a second restart, Redo replays the CLR (re-performing the undo). The Undo phase sees the CLR and jumps over the already-undone records, preventing infinite loops.

Redo Condition

1. P is in DPT. (If not, it was flushed). 
2. recLSN(P) ≤ LSN. (If LSN is older than oldest dirty version, it was flushed). 3. P.pageLSN < LSN. (Check physical disk state).

961/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Recovery Visualization: The ARIES Trace 
Why CLRs Matter (Crash During Recovery) 
■ Imagine system crashes after LSN 100 (Undo of 90 complete, Undo of 60 pending). 
■Restart Redo: Replays 90, then Replays 100 (Redoing the Undo). P3 is safe. 
■Restart Undo: 
■Reads LSN 100 (CLR). 
■ Sees UndoNextLSN = 60. 
■Skips 90. Jumps directly to 60. 
■Result: We never undo the same record twice. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
962/1259 
Logging Granularity 
Concurrency & Recovery Advanced Recovery: Logging Internals 
■The "Log Record" describes a change. But how specific should it be? 
■ Physical Logging: Records exact byte changes at specific offsets. 
■ Example: "On Page 42, Offset 100, write bytes OxAF....' 
■ Pros: Idempotent (Redo is just memcpy). Fast. 
■Cons: Rigid. If the page was reorganized (e.g., compaction) by another transaction, Physical Undo is impossible. 
■Logical Logging: Records the high-level operation. 
■ Example: "Insert Tuple T into Table R." 
■ Pros: Flexible. Undo just deletes T (Logical Inverse). 
■Cons: Slow Redo. Must traverse the B-Tree again to find the page. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
963/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Advanced Recovery: Logging Internals 
Physiological Logging (The ARIES Standard) 
■The Hybrid Approach: Physical-to-a-Page, Logical-within-a-Page. 
■Structure: (PageID, Operation, Data). 
■ Example: "On Page 42, Insert Tuple T into Slot Directory." 
■Why it wins: 
■Restricted Scope: We know exactly which page to load (No tree traversal). 
■Flexibility: Within the page, we can shift bytes (compaction) without breaking 
the log ́s validity, as long as the slot logic holds. 
■ Undo/Redo: ARIES typically uses Physiological for Undo/Redo, ensuring correctness even if B-Tree splits occurred. 
Database Systems 
2025/26/2 
964/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Advanced Recovery: Logging Internals 
Checkpointing Strategies 
■ Checkpoints shorten recovery time by bounding the amount of Redo work. 
1. Consistent (Blocking) Checkpoint 
1. Stop all transactions (Quiesce). 
2. Flush all dirty pages to disk. 
3. Write CHECKPOINT record. 
4. Resume. 
Verdict: Terrible for latency. The "Stop-the-World" pause is unacceptable in high-availability systems. 
2. Fuzzy (Non-Blocking) Checkpoint 
■ System continues running. 
■ We record the state of the Dirty Page Table and Transaction Table in the log. 
We quarantee that any dirty nage older than the checkpoint is eventually flushed (back- 
Database Systems

Concurrency & Recovery Advanced Recovery: Logging Internals 
Media Failure & Archiving 
■ Crash Recovery: Protects against RAM loss (Power failure). 
■ Media Recovery: Protects against Disk loss (Drive failure). 
■ Mechanism: WAL Archiving. 
■ As Log segments (files) fill up, copy them to a separate storage location (S3, Tape, NAS). 
■ Base Backup: Periodically copy the data files (Heap/Index). 
■Restore Procedure: 
1. Restore Base Backup. 
2. Replay Archived WAL files (Redo) to bring the database forward in time.

966/1259 
Concurrency & Recovery Advanced Recovery: Logging Internals 
Point-in-Time Recovery (PITR) 
■ Scenario: A developer accidentally drops a table at 10:00 AM. 
■Goal: Restore the DB to the state at 09:59 AM. 
■ Process: 
1. Restore the last Base Backup (taken at 02:00 AM). 
2. Replay WAL records starting from 02:00 AM. 
3. Stop Logic: Stop replaying when the log timestamp reaches 09:59 AM. 
■ Prerequisite: Continuous WAL Archiving must be enabled.
